(Morse decomposition of a manifold does not usually give the structure a CW-complex), and in its algebro-geometrical version Bialynicki-Birula theory
[
27
]
(the analogues of cell complexes are called affine pavings). The requirement that
|\partial C(\sigma)|
is homeomorphic to a sphere may be weakened in several obvious ways.

Definition A.30
.

A graded poset
(\mathcal{X},\rk)
is called a
homotopy cell poset
if, for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
, the boundary
|\partial C(\sigma)|
is homotopy equivalent to the sphere
S^{\rk\sigma-1}
.

Definition A.31
.

A graded poset
(\mathcal{X},\rk)
is called a
homology cell poset
if, for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
, the boundary
|\partial C(\sigma)|
has the same
\mathbb{Z}
-homology as the sphere
S^{\rk\sigma-1}
:

H_{i}(|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\begin{cases}\cong\mathbb{Z},&\mbox{if }i=\rk\sigma-1\\
=0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Definition
A.31
has an advantage over Definitions
A.30
and
A.26
: the property of a graded poset
(\mathcal{X},\rk)
to be a homology cell poset is algorithmically decidable, while homotopy cell posets and cell posets are not, see Remark
A.20
. Yet another way to weaken the definition of a cell poset is to drop the assumption of being graded. We will see in Subsection
C.7
that the basic properties of cellular sheaves and their cohomology remain the same if these more general notions are used instead of cell posets.

Definition A.32
.

A poset
\mathcal{X}
is called a
(homology) Morse cell poset
if, for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
, the boundary
|\partial C(\sigma)|
has the same singular homology as a sphere
S^{d_{s}-1}
. In this case, the elements
s\in S
are called
cells
, and the corresponding number
d_{s}
is called
the dimension of a cell

s
.

Remark A.33
.

Depending on the context, singular homology is taken with specific coefficients. If the target abelian category is
\mathbf{Abel}=\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{Mod}
, then the coefficients are taken in
\mathbb{Z}
, so that
|\partial C(\sigma)|
should have the same
\mathbb{Z}
-homology as a sphere. If the target abelian category is the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
of vector spaces over a field
\Bbbk
, then
|\partial C(\sigma)|
should have the same
\Bbbk
-homology as a sphere. The same remark relates to Definition
A.31
as well.

The definition of Morse homotopy cell poset may be given in a straightforward way.

Figure 8.
Example of Morse sell poset and the corresponding “topological intuition”. There are two cellular approximations available for this poset.

Example A.34
.

Fig.
8
shows an example of a Morse cell poset which is not graded. There is a geometrical intuition behind such a poset: assume we have 3 vertices
1,2,3
and two edges
a,b
, and
a
is attached to
1
and
2
in a natural way, while endpoints of
b
are attached to some middle-point of
a
and
3
respectively. This geometrical figure is not a CW-complex, because it contradicts to the assumption that boundaries of cells are attached to lower-dimensional skeleta. However, such situations often (if not always) occur in the classical Morse theory: the (un)stable manifolds of the gradient flow of a Morse function do not form a CW-decomposition.

Remark A.35
.

The posets defined by either of the Definitions (
A.26
),
A.30
,
A.31
, or
A.32
will be called cellular-like posets. If
\mathcal{X}
is cellular-like, then lower order ideals
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
will be called cell subposets. The condition of lower order ideal means that whenever
\sigma\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\tau<\sigma
, then
\tau\in\mathcal{Y}
. It is easily seen that cell subposets inherit the defining property of
\mathcal{X}
: for example, if
\mathcal{X}
is a homology Morse cell poset, then so are its subposets
\mathcal{Y}
.

A convention used in the following: notation
S_{\leq s}
,
S_{<s}
is used for generic posets, while
C(s)
,
\partial C(s)
is used for cell posets to be defined below. The letter
C
stands here for “cell”, and cell is usually assumed to be bounded by a sphere, or something like a sphere.

Appendix B
Sheaves and diagrams

B.1.
Diagrams

In this section we recall the notion of a diagram on a poset, sheaf on a topological space, and describe their basic relations. We start with a more common and more intuitive notion of a diagram on a poset.

We recall some basic terminology from category theory to fix the notation. If
\mathbf{V}
is a category,
\Ob(\mathbf{V})
denotes the class of objects of
\mathbf{V}
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{V}}(a,b)
the set of morphisms from an object
a
to an object
b
. We usually abuse the notation and write
a\in\mathbf{V}
instead of
a\in\Ob(\mathbf{V})
. The symbol
\mathbf{C}
will denotes generic small categories, while
\mathbf{V}
is reserved for big categories such as

(1)

the category
\mathbf{Sets}
of sets and their maps;

(2)

the category
\mathbf{Abel}
of abelian groups and group homomorphisms;

(3)

the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
of vector spaces over a ground field
\Bbbk
and linear maps;

(4)

the category
R\mathbf{Mod}
of
R
-modules and module homomorphisms.

Usually it is assumed that
\mathbf{V}
is
complete and cocomplete
, i.e. has all direct and inverse limits.

Definition B.1
.

A
diagram on a preposet

S
valued in a category
\mathbf{V}
is a functor
D\colon\mathbf{cat}(S)\to\mathbf{V}
.

Remark B.2
.

In simple words, a diagram on a preposet consists of two assignments. (1) To each element
s\in S
we assign some object
D(s)=V_{s}
of a category
\mathbf{V}
(e.g., if the category
\mathbf{V}
is
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
,
V_{s}
is a vector space). (2) To each pair
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
such that
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
, we assign a map

f_{s_{1}s_{2}}=D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})\colon V_{s_{1}}\to V_{s_{2}}.

These maps satisfy compositionality:
f_{ss}=\id_{V_{s}}
for any
s\in S
, and
f_{s_{1}s_{2}};f_{s_{2}s_{3}}=f_{s_{1}s_{3}}
for any
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
.

All diagrams on
S
form the category
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
, in which the morphisms are natural transformations of functors.

Remark B.3
.

Let
S
be a preordered set, and
\bar{S}
the corresponding quotient poset, as described in Construction
A.4
. Assume
D
is a diagram on
S
. Whenever
s_{1}\sim s_{2}
in
S
, we have two maps

D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})\colon D(s_{1})\leftrightarrows D(s_{2})\colon D(s_{2}\leq s_{1})

which are inverses of each other, hence provide the isomorphism between
D(s_{1})
and
D(s_{2})
. Therefore we can meaningfully define a diagram
\bar{D}
on
\bar{S}=S/\sim
by setting
\bar{D}([s])=D(s)
,
\bar{D}([s_{1}]\leq[s_{2}])=D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})
. Two maps
S\leftrightarrows\bar{S}
described in Construction
A.4
prove equivalence of categories
\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{Diag}(\bar{S};\mathbf{V})
. For this reason, when we speak of diagrams, sheaves or whatever, we can restrict consideration to posets. Every preordered set can be naturally transformed into a poset so that the category of diagrams do not change.

B.2.
Presheaves and sheaves

Now we define the notion of a sheaf and presheaf. For a general topological space
X
let
\OpSets(X)
denote the set of all open subsets of
X
. The set
\OpSets(X)
is partially ordered by inclusion.

Definition B.4
.

A
presheaf
on a topological space
X
valued in
\mathbf{V}
is a contravariant functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbf{cat}(\OpSets(X)\setminus\{\varnothing\})^{\op}\to\mathbf{V}
.

Remark B.5
.

\mathbf{cat}(\OpSets(X)\setminus\{\varnothing\})^{\op}
is a category, opposite to the category of the poset of open subsets of
X
. In other words, presheaf can be considered as a rule which associates, to any non-empty open subset
U\in\Omega_{X}
, an object
V_{U}=\mathcal{F}(U)
of the category
\mathbf{V}
, and, to each pair of open subsets
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
, a morphism

r_{U_{2}\supseteq U_{1}}=\mathcal{F}(U_{2}\supseteq U_{1})\colon V_{U_{2}}\to V_{U_{1}}.

These maps are called
restriction morphisms
of a presheaf. All restriction morphisms should satisfy the compositionality conditions:
r_{U\supseteq U}=\id_{V_{U}}
and
r_{U_{3}\supseteq U_{2}};r_{U_{2}\supseteq U_{1}}=r_{U_{3}\supseteq U_{1}}
.

Remark B.6
.

In the common categories
\mathbf{V}
like
\mathbf{Sets}
or
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
, the notion of an element of an object
V\in\Ob(\mathbf{V})
make sense. The elements
x\in\mathcal{F}(U)
are called
sections
of a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on a set
U
. If
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
and
x\in\mathcal{F}(U_{2})
, then
\mathcal{F}(U_{2}\supseteq U_{1})(x)\in\mathcal{F}(U_{1})
is called the restriction of the section
x
to the subset
U_{1}
and is denoted by
x|_{U_{1}}
.

Definition B.7
.

A presheaf
\mathcal{F}
is called a
sheaf
if the following two properties are satisfied:

(1)

(Locality) Let
U
be an open set, which is covered by a family of open subsets,
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
U_{i}\subseteq U
for each
i\in I
, and suppose we are given two elements
s,t\in\mathcal{F}(U)
. If
s|_{U_{i}}=t|_{U_{i}}
for each
i\in I
, then
s=t
.

(2)

(Gluing) Let
U
be an open set and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be its open covering by subsets
U_{i}\subseteq U
and let
\{s_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of sections. Suppose the sections agree on the overlap of their domains, i.e.
s_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=s_{j}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
for each
i,j\in I
. Then there exists a section
s\in\mathcal{F}(U)
such that
s|_{U_{i}}=s_{i}
for each
i\in I
.

All
\mathbf{V}
-valued presheaves on a topology
X
form the category
\mathbf{PreShvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
, whose morphisms are natural transformations of functors from
\mathbf{cat}(\OpSets(X)\setminus\{\varnothing\})^{\op}
to
\mathbf{V}
. Sheaves form a complete subcategory
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
in the category
\mathbf{PreShvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
.

Definition B.8
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a presheaf on a topological space
X
and
x\in X
, then

\mathcal{F}_{x}=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\
U\in\Omega_{X},U\ni x\end{subarray}}\mathcal{F}(U)\hskip 6.0pt\in\Ob(\mathbf{V})

(the direct limit of values of the presheaf over all open subsets which contain
x
), is called a
stalk
of the presheaf
\mathcal{F}
at a point
x
.

The definition makes sense only if the category
\mathbf{V}
has direct limits. From the general categorical definition of a direct limit
[
140
]
it follows that for each
U\ni x
,
U\in\Omega_{X}
there exists a canonical morphism
\mathcal{F}(U\ni x)\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathcal{F}_{x}
.

B.3.
Relation between sheaves and diagrams

Apparently, diagrams on posets and sheaves on topologies are connected via the notion Alexandrov topology and Kan’s extension. According to Proposition
A.7
, each poset
S
corresponds to a
T_{0}
-Alexandrov topology
X_{S}
. There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between diagrams on
S
valued in
\mathbf{V}
and sheaves on
X_{S}
valued in
\mathbf{V}
.

Proposition B.9
.

The category
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
of diagrams on a poset
S
is naturally equivalent to the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})
of sheaves on the corresponding Alexandrov topology.

For completenes, we review two standard constructions providing transformation of a diagram into a sheaf and vice versa.

Construction B.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a presheaf on
X_{S}
. Recall that for each point
x\in S
there is a minimal open neighbourhood
U_{x}
in the topology on
X_{S}
(see Construction
A.9
and Remark
A.10
). If
x\leq y
, then we have
U_{x}\supseteq U_{y}
. Define a diagram
F
on
S
by

F(x)=\mathcal{F}(U_{x}),\qquad F(x\leq y)=\mathcal{F}(U_{x}\supseteq U_{y}).

It is easy to check that this diagram on
S
is well-defined.

Remark B.11
.

For a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on the Alexandrov topology we have
\mathcal{F}_{x}=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\
x\in U\end{subarray}}\mathcal{F}(U)=\mathcal{F}(U_{x})
since
U_{x}
is the minimal open set containing a point
x
, thus is the terminal object of the diagram, on which the limit is taken. Therefore, Construction
B.10
means that
F
is actually the diagram of stalks of a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
.

Construction B.12
.

Let
D
be a diagram on
S
. We construct the corresponding sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on the Alexandrov space
X_{S}
with
\Omega_{S}=\OpSets(X_{S})
the collection of upper order ideals. For each open set
U\in\Omega_{S}
define

(B.1)

\mathcal{D}(U)=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\\
s\in U\end{subarray}}D(s),

— the inverse limit of the restriction of the diagram
D
to a subcategory
\mathbf{cat}(U)
. For each pair
U_{1}\subset U_{2}
we define the map
\mathcal{D}(U_{2}\supset U_{1})\colon\mathcal{D}(U_{2})\to\mathcal{D}(U_{1})
in a natural way, by the universal property of the inverse limit. The uniqueness of the universal object and the corresponding morphisms guarantee that
\mathcal{D}
is a presheaf on
X_{S}
(i.e. a commutative diagram on
\mathbf{cat}(\Omega_{S}\setminus\{\varnothing\})^{\op}
). Moreover, simple arguments involving the uniqueness and naturality of the inverse limit establish the properties of locality and gluing, which ensure that
\mathcal{D}
is a sheaf on
X_{S}
. We call it
the sheaf of sections
of the diagram
D
.

Remark B.13
.

In more abstract terms, Construction
B.12
can be defined by the right Kan extension, see
[
64
, Theorem 4.2.10]
.

Checking that Constructions
B.10
and
B.12
define the bijective correspondence between sheaves on
X_{S}
and diagrams on
S
is yet another simple exercise for the reader. Functoriality of both constructions is easily checked easily as well proving Proposition
B.9
.

Remark B.14
.

We note that Construction
B.10
is formulated for an arbitrary presheaf
\mathcal{F}
. Let us take a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on an Alexandrov space
X
and build the diagram
F
of its stalks by Construction
B.10
, and then build its sheaf of sections
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
by Construction
B.12
. Then the sheaf
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
has the same stalks as the presheaf
\mathcal{F}
. Generally speaking, a sheaf with such properties is called the
sheafification
of a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
. It is the left adjoint functor of the inclusion functor from
\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{V})
to
\mathbf{PreShvs}(X;\mathbf{V})
.

B.4.
Global sections

Construction B.15
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})
be a sheaf. The value

\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}(X_{S})\in\mathbf{V}

of this sheaf on the whole space
X_{S}
is called the set
19
19

19

Formally it is not the set, but rather an object of a category
\mathbf{V}
. However, since most common examples of categories
\mathbf{V}
are concrete, it is not a big mistake to call it a set.
of global sections of the sheaf
\mathcal{F}
. From the definition of a sheaf it follows that global sections are functorial: if we have a morphism
f\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{F}
of sheaves, we naturally have a map
\Gamma(X_{S};f)\colon\mathcal{D}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{F}(X_{S})
of their values. Henceforth, we have a functor

(B.2)

\Gamma(X_{S},\cdot)\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{V},

which is called
the functor of global sections
.

Construction B.16
.

Proposition
B.9
states
\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
. In the language of diagrams over
S
, we have the following description of the global sections. Let
D\in\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
be a diagram. Then
\Gamma(S,D)
is the value of the corresponding sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on the space
X_{S}
. This value is defined as the inverse limit

\Gamma(S,D)=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\\
s\in S\end{subarray}}D(s)

according to the general Construction
B.12
. This is the reason why the functor of global sections is called
the inverse limit functor
in topological and algebraical sources, see
[
47
,
96
]
.

Remark B.17
.

We provide an explicit construction of the inverse limit in the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
in order to make formula (
B.1
) more meaningful for readers not familiar with abstract nonsense. Essentially the procedure of taking inverse limits is the most essential ingredient in the applications of sheaves: this part of the theory deals with solving equations. We have

(B.3)

\mathcal{D}(U)=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\\
s\in U\end{subarray}}D(s)=\left\{(x_{s}\mid s\in U)\in\prod\nolimits_{s\in U}D(s)\mid D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})x_{s_{1}}=x_{s_{2}}\text{ for any }s_{1}\leq s_{2}\right\}.

In other words, a section
x\in\mathcal{D}(U)
on an open set
U
coincides with a collection of vectors
x_{s}\in D(s)
, one for each
s\in U
, which are compatible with respect to the maps of the diagram. We also call such collection of vectors coherent. In the form (
B.3
), the restriction map
\mathcal{D}(U_{2}\supset U_{1})
is written in an straightforward manner, because the family of compatible stalks on the set
U_{2}
is also compatible on its subset
U_{1}
.

We give an important methodological remark, to which we refer in the analysis of the sheaves’ applications.

Remark B.18
.

A construction similar to (
B.3
) makes sense in other common categories such us
\mathbf{Sets}
,
R\mathbf{Mod}
,
\mathbf{Top}
, etc. In more general categorical terms, there is a standard statement (see e.g.
[
140
, §V.2]
) that procedure of taking inverse limits (over small or finite, respectively, categories) reduces to taking small or finite, respectively, products and equalizers. In practice, this means that dealing with general data-structures
\mathbf{V}
as the values of a (finitely supported) sheaf
\mathcal{F}
, the generation of a global section
\mathcal{F}(U)
reduces to two procedures as follows.

(1)

Generation of finite products. Usually not a problem, since Cartesian products are supported by all common data structures. E.g. concatenation of vectors is ubiquitous in data science, and is rarely explicitly mentioned at all.

(2)

Generation of equalizers. In practice this means solving (systems of) equations of the form “Find
x\in V
, such that
F(x)=G(x)
” where
V,W\in\Ob(\mathbf{V})
and
F,G\in\Hom(V,W)
are given. This step is usually hard, but it makes the theory substantial. The solution of equations (as a process) is the substantial part of sheaves’ inspired architectures of neural networks, as defined in subsection
2.7
and reviewed in Section
4
.

B.5.
Functorial properties

Here we discuss how the maps of spaces and posets interact with sheaves and diagrams. We won’t retell the whole six-functor formalism of Grothendieck here and restrict to the necessary minimum. In general, a continuous map of topological spaces
f\colon X\to Y
induces direct and inverse image functors on categories of sheaves

f_{*}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Shvs}(Y;\mathbf{V}),\qquad f^{*}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(Y;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{V}).

For the details of this construction in general, we refer to
[
116
]
. In case of posets the definition of the functor
f^{*}
(inverse image of a sheaf) is significantly simplified at the conceptual level and, as a result, finds numerous applications in applied topology and contemporary approaches to neural networks.

Let
f\colon S\to T
be a morphism of posets (equiv, a continuous map of the corresponding Alexandrov topological spaces), see Proposition
A.7
.

Definition B.19
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})
be a sheaf on the Alexandrov space
X_{S}
. Then its
direct image
is a presheaf
f_{*}\mathcal{F}
on the topological space
X_{T}
whose values on open subsets
U\in\OpSets(X_{S})
and inclusions
U_{1}\supseteq U_{2}
are defined by the following formulas:

f_{*}\mathcal{F}(U)=\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}(U)),\qquad f_{*}\mathcal{F}(U_{1}\supseteq U_{2})=\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}(U_{1})\supseteq f^{-1}(U_{2})).

A quite standard proposition states that the direct image
f_{*}\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf. The map
f_{*}
is functorial, i.e. it is a well-defined functor from the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})
to the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T};\mathbf{V})
. The inverse image functor is more intricate, since an image of an open set under a continuous map may fail to be open. However, for posets and Alexandrov topologies, the construction of the inverse image functor is simplified due to the equivalence between sheaves and diagrams over posets. Assume again that
f\colon S\to T
is a morphism of posets and
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T};\mathbf{V})
is a sheaf on the topological space
X_{T}
. According to Proposition
B.9
, sheaf
\mathcal{D}
corresponds to a diagram
D\colon\mathbf{cat}(T)\to\mathbf{V}
.

Definition B.20
.

The
inverse image
of a diagram
D
on
T
(or the corresponding sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on
X_{T}
) under a morphism
f\colon S\to T
is
a diagram
f^{*}D
on the poset
S
(or the corresponding sheaf
f^{*}\mathcal{D}
on a space
X_{S}
), defined by the formulas
20
20

20

In algebraic geometry there is a difference between
f^{-1}
and
f^{*}
, and here we are actually defining
f^{-1}
. Since we do not work with ringed spaces in this review, we prefer to keep notation simple and use symbol
f^{*}
for inverse image.
:

f^{*}D(s)=D(f(s)),\qquad f^{*}D(s\leq s^{\prime})=D(f(s)\leq f(s^{\prime})).

It can be seen that
f^{*}
is functorial, so that we have a functor
f^{*}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})
. The proof of the next statement can be found in
[
64
, Theorem 5.3.1]
for sheaves on finite spaces, or in
[
116
, Theorem 4.8]
for general topological spaces.

Proposition B.21
.

The functors of direct and inverse image form an adjoint pair. This means there is a natural bijection

\Hom_{\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T},\mathbf{V})}(\mathcal{D},f_{*}\mathcal{F})=\Hom_{\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})}(f^{*}\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})

for any
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})
and
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T},\mathbf{V})
.

Recalling Proposition
B.9
, we have a sequence of natural bijections

(B.4)

\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(T,\mathbf{V})}(D,f_{*}F)=\Hom_{\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{T},\mathbf{V})}(\mathcal{D},f_{*}\mathcal{F})=\\
\Hom_{\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})}(f^{*}\mathcal{D},\mathcal{F})=\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})}(f^{*}D,F).

Appendix C
Sheaf cohomology

This section describes the definition of sheaf cohomology, and introduces various computational approaches to cohomology calculations. An important role here is played by additive, and more generally abelian categories, and additive functors between them. The basic examples of abelian categories are the categories
\mathbf{Abel}
of abelian groups, the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
of vector spaces over a field
\Bbbk
, or the category
R\mathbf{Mod}
of modules (e.g. left modules) over a ring
R
. For a general introduction to abelian categories we refer to e.g.
[
86
]
. We are mainly interested in Grothendieck categories, the particularly well-behaved subclass of abelian categories, to which the listed examples belong
[
39
, Ch.5,SS8-9]
.

C.1.
Recap on (co)chain complexes

In this subsection we review the basic toolbox of homological algebra: cochain complexes and exact sequences.

Definition C.1
.

Assume
\mathbf{V}
is an abelian category. The sequence

(C.1)

0\to C^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{0}}}{{\to}}C^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\to}}C^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}\cdots

of objects of
\mathbf{V}
and morphisms between them is called a
(connective) cochain complex
21
21

21

We consider only connective cochain complexes, i.e. indexed with nonnegative integers, because only these complexes appear in the review.
if
d_{j};d_{j+1}=0
for any
j=0,1,\ldots
(sometimes the numeration starts with
-1
). For short, a cochain complex (
C.1
) is denoted
(C^{*},d)
.

The definition implies the natural inclusion
p_{j}\colon\im d_{j}\hookrightarrow\Ker d_{j+1}
.

Definition C.2
.

The
cohomology of the cochain complex

(C^{*},d)
are defined as the objects

H^{j}(C^{*},d)=\Ker d_{j+1}/\im d_{j}=\Coker p_{j}\mbox{ for }j=0,1,\ldots

of the category
\mathbf{V}
, indexed by nonnegative numbers.

A cochain complex
(C^{*},d)
— or any sequence of the form (
C.1
) — is called
exact
(resp. exact at position
i
), if
H^{j}(C^{*},d)=0
for any
j
(resp. for
j=i
). Equivalently,
\im d_{j}=\Ker d_{j+1}
.

Construction C.3
.

Connective cochain complexes over
\mathbf{V}
form a category, in which the maps (called
cochain maps
) are defined componentwise. A cochain map
f\colon(C^{*},d_{C})\to(B^{*},d_{B})
is defined if the maps
f_{j}\colon C^{j}\to B^{j}
are defined for any
j
, commuting with the differentials
f_{j};d_{b}=d_{C};f_{j+1}
. Each such map induces the map of cohomology
f_{*}\colon H^{j}(C^{*},d_{C})\to H^{j}(B^{*},d_{B})
. Let us denote the category of cochain complexes over
\mathbf{V}
by
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
. The category
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
is an abelian category itself with kernels and cokernels defined componentwise in a straightforward manner.

The following lemma is classical, see e.g.
[
198
, Lemma 1.3.2]
.

Lemma C.4

(Zig-zag lemma)
.

A short exact sequence

0\to(C^{*},d_{C})\to(B^{*},d_{B})\to(A^{*},d_{A})\to 0

in
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
induces the long exact sequence of cohomology

(C.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(C^{*},d_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(B^{*},d_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(A^{*},d_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(C^{*},d_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(B^{*},d_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(A^{*},d_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(C^{*},d_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(B^{*},d_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(A^{*},d_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

C.2.
Sheaf cohomology as derived functors

Sheaf cohomology, as they are understood in algebraic geometry and homological algebra, are the derived functor of the global sections’ functor. In this subsection we give the necessary definitions, constructions, and explain the specifics of finite topological spaces in terms of this definition. This subsection is entirely covered by Curry’s thesis
[
64
]
: it is provided here for convenience and also to fix the notation for some important objects to be defined later.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be an abelian category. Then, for any topological space
X
, the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
is an abelian category as well. If
X=X_{S}
is the Alexandrov space corresponding to a poset
S
, then we have an abelian category of diagrams
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})
(see Propositions
A.7
and
B.9
).

Construction C.5
.

In terms of diagrams over posets, the characteristic properties of the abelian category
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
are the following. As the illustrative example one can imagine diagrams of vector spaces
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})
.

(1)

The
zero diagram

0\in\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
is the diagram made of zero vector spaces and zero maps.

(2)

There exist finite
biproducts
, also called
direct sums
. If
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
are two diagrams, then their direct sum
D_{1}\oplus D_{2}
is defined component-wise:

(D_{1}\oplus D_{2})(s)=D_{1}(s)\oplus D_{2}(s)

and

(D_{1}\oplus D_{2})(s\leq t)=D_{1}(s\leq t)\oplus D_{2}(s\leq t).

(3)

Kernel
and
cokernel
of a morphism of diagrams are defined component-wise. If
G\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
is a morphism of diagrams, then the kernel
K\colon\Ker G\hookrightarrow D_{1}
is defined by

(\Ker G)(s)=\Ker G(s),\mbox{ the maps }(\Ker G)(s\leq t)\colon\Ker G(s)\to\Ker G(t)

are naturally induced by
D_{1}(s\leq t)
, and
K(s)\colon(\Ker G)(s)\hookrightarrow D_{1}(s)
is a natural inclusion. Cokernels are completely similar.

(4)

Every monomorphism is a kernel of its cokernel, and every epimorphism is a cokernel of its kernel. Again, easily verified component-wise.

The next proposition follows from the 3-rd item of Construction
C.5
. It constitutes an important property of diagrams over posets: exactness of sequences of sheaves can be verified stalk-wise.

Proposition C.6
.

A sequence
0\to D_{0}\to D_{1}\to D_{2}\to\cdots
of diagrams over a poset
S
is exact if and only if the corresponding sequence of stalks

0\to D_{0}(s)\to D_{1}(s)\to D_{2}(s)\to\cdots

is exact for each
s\in S
.

Sheaves have enough injectives.
In order to deal with derived functors, one needs to assure that the abelian category of sheaves has enough injectives. For a general definition of injective object in an abelian category see
[
198
, §2.3]
. An abelian category
\mathbf{A}
is said to
have enough injectives
if, for any object
A\in\mathbf{A}
there exists a monomorphism from
A
to an injective object of
\mathbf{A}
. The categories
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
,
\mathbf{Abel}
,
R\mathbf{Mod}
are known to have enough injectives
[
198
, §2.3]
. For example, in the “most applied” category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
every object is injective. This simplifies many arguments and constructions in this case.

Proposition C.7

(
[
96
, Thm.1.10.1 and Prop.3.1.1]
)
.

If
\mathbf{V}
has enough injectives, then
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
also has enough injectives.

Below, we provide the proof for finite topologies, following Curry
[
64
, Def.7.1.3]
.

Construction C.8
.

Let
s\in S
and
V\in\mathbf{V}
. Consider the diagram
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil V}}
on
S
, defined by

(C.3)

{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil V}}(t)=\begin{cases}V,&\mbox{if }t\leqslant s\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Mappings between the non-zero elements of the diagram are set to be the identity isomorphisms. The diagrams of this form, and their corresponding sheaves, will be called
cone-shaped sheaves
on
S
(with apex
s
and value
V
). Notice the analogy with the definition of the cone of an element, Construction
A.24
. It can be seen that
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil V}}
coincides with
the skyscraper sheaf
on
X_{S}
, concentrated in
s
, known in the classical homological algebra
[
198
, Ex.2.3.12]
.

Lemma C.9
.

If
W
is an injective object in
\mathbf{V}
, then
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
is an injective object in
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
(equiv. an injective sheaf on the space
X_{S}
).

Proof.

Let
\pt
be the one-point poset. The category of sheaves on
\pt
can be naturally identified with the category
\mathbf{V}
. Thus,
W
can be considered as injective sheaf on
\pt
. Consider the inclusion map
i_{s}\colon\pt\to S
,
i_{s}(\pt)=s
. It is easily verified that
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}=(i_{s})_{*}W
. Hence

(C.4)

\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})}(F,{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})=\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})}(F,(i_{s})_{*}W)=\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(\pt,\mathbf{V})}(i_{s}^{*}F,W)=\Hom_{\mathbf{V}}(F(s),W).

It is known
[
198
, Lm.2.3.4]
that an object
I
of an abelian category
\mathbf{A}
is injective if and only if the functor
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}(\cdot,I)
is exact. Taking stalks at the point
s
is an exact functor from
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
to
\mathbf{V}
, see Proposition
C.6
. Therefore, since
W
is an injective object of
\mathbf{V}
, the functor

(\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V}))\mapsto\Hom_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathcal{F}(s),W)=\Hom_{\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})}(\mathcal{F},{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})

is exact, hence the diagram (the sheaf)
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
is injective.
∎

Proposition
C.7
is proved by the following construction, which is a particular case of the Godement’s construction
[
93
]
applied to a finite topology
22
22

22

It should be noticed that general Godement’s construction works with direct products which, for infinite indexing sets, do not coincide with direct sums, or coproducts. Therefore the assumption of finiteness of
S
is important in this and subsequent constructions.
.

Construction C.10
.

Let
D\in\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
be an arbitrary diagram on a poset. For each element
s\in S
consider a monomorphism
j_{s}\colon D(s)\to J_{s}
to some injective object of
\mathbf{V}
(which exists since we assumed
\mathbf{V}
has enough injectives). Consider the following map

(C.5)

j\colon D\to\bigoplus_{s\in S}{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil J_{s}}},\quad j=\bigoplus_{s\in S}\tilde{j}_{s},

where
\tilde{j}_{s}\colon D\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil J_{s}}}
is the map which corresponds to
j_{s}\colon D(s)\to J_{s}
via the natural correspondence described in (
C.4
). The map
j
is monomorphic stalk-wise: it is monomorphic on an element
s
since already its summand
\tilde{j}_{s}
(which is basically
j_{s}
) is monomorphic.

In short, we mapped a diagram
D
into the direct sum of the cone-shaped diagrams valued by objects in which the values of
D
inject.

Remark C.11
.

Construction
C.10
asserts, that every sheaf embeds monomorphically into a direct sum of cone-shaped sheaves, which is injective. In the following, when we speak about injective sheaves on Alexandrov topologies, we usually mean “sums of cone-shaped sheaves” since no other concrete constructions of injective sheaves appear in our work.

Derived functors.
The most important functor in sheaf theory is the functor of global sections described in Construction
B.15
. If
\mathbf{V}
is abelian, then the functor of global sections
\Gamma(X_{S};\cdot)
(or
\Gamma(S;\cdot)=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow,S\end{subarray}}(\cdot)
in terms of posets) can be seen to be additive.

In homological algebra, given two sufficiently nice abelian categories
\mathbf{W}
,
\mathbf{V}
and an additive functor
\mathfrak{F}
between them, it is possible to define an infinite sequence of functors, called the derived functors of
\mathfrak{F}
. Some steps of this definition appear useful in the following, so we recall the abstract definitions with a moderate level of mathematical details. The main purpose of this rather classical description is to check its algorithmical feasibility for finite diagrams of finite-dimensional vector spaces.

Definition C.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{V}
be an additive functor between two abelian categories. The functor
\mathfrak{F}
is called
left exact
if every short exact sequence in
\mathbf{W}

0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W_{3}\to 0

induces the sequence

(C.6)

0\to\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})\to\mathfrak{F}(W_{2})\to\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})

which is exact at all positions except the rightmost.

Assume that
\mathbf{W}
has enough injectives. Let us define the right derived functors
R^{j}\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{V}
. Actually, we will concentrate on constructing
R^{j}\mathfrak{F}(W)
for a given object
W\in\mathbf{W}
, not a morphism. The tedious check of functoriality is based on the horseshoe lemma and may be found elsewhere
[
198
, Theorem 2.3.7]
.

Construction C.13
.

Let
W\in\mathbf{W}
be an object. Since
\mathbf{W}
has enough injectives, there exists a monomorphic map
\imath_{0}\colon M\to I_{0}
into some injective object. Now let us take an injective hull of
\Coker\imath_{0}
, which gives a map
\imath_{1}\colon I_{0}\to I_{1}
. Proceeding the same way inductively, we get a potentially infinite exact sequence

(C.7)

0\to W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0}}}{{\to}}I_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{1}}}{{\to}}I_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{2}}}{{\to}}I_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{3}}}{{\to}}\cdots

in which all objects
I_{j}
are injective. Such a sequence is called an
injective resolution
of
W
; it may be non-unique. Now apply the functor
\mathfrak{F}
to the sequence (
C.7
) cut at the leftmost position:

(C.8)

0\to\mathfrak{F}(I_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}(i_{1})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{F}(I_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}(i_{2})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{F}(I_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}(i_{3})}}{{\to}}\cdots

This sequence may fail to be exact, but it is a cochain complex in
\mathbf{V}
. The cohomology of this cochain complex (see Definition
C.2
) are called the
values of right derived functors
of
\mathfrak{F}
on
W
:

R^{j}\mathfrak{F}(W)=H^{j}(\mathfrak{F}(I^{*},\imath)).

For convenience we provide the list of basic claims about derived functors in general, the proofs can be found e.g. in
[
198
, §2.5]
.

Proposition C.14
.

The following properties hold for the derived functors
R^{j}\mathfrak{F}
.

(1)

R^{j}\mathfrak{F}(W)
are well-defined up to natural isomorphism. This basically means that these objects of
\mathbf{V}
do not depend on the choice of injective resolution of
W
.

(2)

R^{0}\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}
.

(3)

Each short exact sequence
0\to W_{1}\to W_{2}\to W_{3}\to 0
in
\mathbf{W}
induces the long exact sequence

(C.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}\mathfrak{F}(W_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}\mathfrak{F}(W_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{1}\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}\mathfrak{F}(W_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{1}\mathfrak{F}(W_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}\mathfrak{F}(W_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{3}\mathfrak{F}(W_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

which extends the sequence (
C.6
).

Sheaf cohomology.
In order for the definition
2.28
of sheaf cohomology to make sense, a bit more preparatory work should be done.

Proposition C.15
.

The functor
\Gamma(S,\cdot)
is left exact.

This is derived from the fact that
\Gamma(S;\cdot)
is right adjoint to the constant sheaf functor, see
[
198
, §2.6]
. The abelian category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})
has enough injectives by Proposition
C.7
, therefore the derived functors of the functor
\Gamma(X_{S};\cdot)
are well defined according to Construction
C.13
. The next definition duplicates Definition
2.28
given in the main part of the text.

Definition C.16
.

The
j
-th right derived functor
R^{j}\Gamma(X_{S};\cdot)
is called
the functor of
j
-th cohomology of a sheaf
. We use the notation
23
23

23

The notation AG stands for algebraic geometry. It may rightfully refer to Alexander Grothendieck.

H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{F})=R^{j}\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F}).

Applying Proposition
C.14
to the functor
\Gamma(X_{S};\cdot)\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{V}
of global sections, we obtain the main properties of the sheaf cohomology.

Proposition C.17
.

Consider sheaves on the Alexandrov topology
X_{S}
corresponding to a poset
S
. The following properties hold true for the cohomology.

(1)

Cohomology modules
H_{AG}^{j}(X_{S};\mathcal{F})
are well-defined and functorial with respect to a sheaf-component.

(2)

The 0-degree cohomology
H_{AG}^{0}(X_{S};\mathcal{F})
coincide with the global sections
\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F})
.

(3)

Each short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to\mathcal{F}_{2}\to\mathcal{F}_{3}\to 0
of sheaves over
X_{S}
induces long exact sequence in cohomology:

(C.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{1}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{1}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{1}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{2}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{2}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{AG}^{2}(X_{S};\mathcal{F}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Next, we provide more constructive details on injective resolutions of sheaves over Alexandrov spaces and their cohomology.

Construction C.18
.

Assume
D
is a diagram over a poset
S
(equiv. sheaf
\mathcal{D}
over the Alexandrov space
X_{S}
), and as before,
\mathbf{V}
is an abelian category with enough injectives. In the abelian category
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S},\mathbf{V})
there exists an injective resolution

(C.11)

0\to\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{I}_{0}\to\mathcal{I}_{1}\to\mathcal{I}_{3}\to\cdots

that is an exact sequence of sheaves, in which all
\mathcal{I}_{j}
are injective. Moreover, according to Construction
C.10
we may assume that each
\mathcal{I}_{j}
is a direct sum of the sheaves of the form
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
with
s\in S
and
W
an injective object of
\mathbf{V}
. Now we apply the functor of global sections to the non-augmented resolution:

(C.12)

0\to\mathcal{D}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{I}_{0}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{I}_{1}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{I}_{2}(X_{S})\to\cdots

which, in the language of posets, the same as the following sequence

(C.13)

0\to\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}D\to\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}I_{0}\to\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}I_{1}\to\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}I_{2}\to\cdots

according to Construction
B.15
. The sequence (
C.12
) is obviously a cochain complex in
\mathbf{V}
. Its cohomology are called the cohomology of the sheaf
\mathcal{D}
, or cohomology of category
\mathbf{cat}(S)
with coefficients in a functor
D
(or simply cohomology of a diagram
D
):

(C.14)

H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})=H^{j}_{AG}(S;D)=\Ker(d\colon\mathcal{I}_{j}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{I}_{j+1}(X_{S}))/\im(d\colon\mathcal{I}_{j-1}(X_{S})\to\mathcal{I}_{j}(X_{S})).

In view of this construction, it is useful to compute the global sections of the basic “injective block”
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
used in the construction of injective resolution.

Lemma C.19
.

For a sheaf
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
defined in Construction
C.8
, the global sections
\Gamma(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})={{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(X_{S})
are naturally isomorphic to
W
. Moreover, if
s\leq t
in
S
and the map
g\colon{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}\to{{}_{\lceil}t_{\rceil V}}
is induced by
f\colon W\to V
, that is defined on stalks by

g(s^{\prime})=\begin{cases}f\colon W={{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(s^{\prime})\to V={{}_{\lceil}t_{\rceil V}},&\mbox{if }s^{\prime}\leq s\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

then the induced map
g_{*}\colon\Gamma(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})\to\Gamma(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}t_{\rceil V}})
coincides with
f\colon W\to V
.

The proof is a simple exercise: on the universality of an inverse limit if it is proved in an arbitrary category, or on the application of Remark
B.17
if one prefers to stay concrete.

Remark C.20
.

It follows from Construction
C.18
, that higher cohomology
H^{>0}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})
of the basic injective sheaf vanish. Indeed, an injective resolution of
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
has the form

0\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}\to 0\to 0\to\cdots

so the global sections of its reduced version give the cochain complex
0\to W\to 0\to\cdots
, with no higher order cohomology.

In subsection
2.5
it was explained that sheaf cohomology provide a language to speak about ways of information distribution and copying in a network. In terms of these allusion, the skyscraper sheaf
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
encodes the situation, when we have a single node
s\in S
, the opinion leader, which possesses information
W
, and all lower nodes of a poset copy this information identically (follow the leader). A consensus state of such sheaf is determined entirely by a state
x_{s}\in{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(s)=W
of the opinion leader hence we have
H^{0}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})=\Gamma(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})\cong W
as stated in Lemma
C.19
. Each node
t
receives its state in
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(t)
in a single way: by copying it from
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(s)
if
t\leq s
, or by setting it to
0
otherwise. Therefore, higher cohomology of
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
vanish. Although the implication “injective
\Rightarrow
acyclic” is mathematically tautological, it is fairly consistent with the common-sense interpretation demonstrated in Example
2.27
.

The procedure of constructing injective resolution can be informally understood as a decomposition of an arbitrary medium (sheaf)
D
on
S
into a combination of simpler media
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
: these media have opinion leaders hence are easier to deal with
24
24

24

This certainly looks like a good reason for political engineers to start using injective resolutions in their work.
.

Remark C.21
.

Assume that
S
is finite, and
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{Abel}
is the category of abelian groups. Is it possible to algorithmically compute cohomology of a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X_{S}
? Potentially, yes, Construction
C.18
describes the algorithm. However, there is one formal detail, which complicates the deal. Namely, we should specify a computationally feasible class of abelian groups. In topological practice, consideration is restricted to finitely generated abelian groups. However, unwinding Constructions
C.18
and
C.10
, we see the necessity to generate injective hulls in
\mathbf{V}
, which kicks us out of the class of finitely generated groups. For example, the injective hull of the simplest group
\mathbb{Z}
is
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}
so we need to take the groups like
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, their direct sums, and homomorphisms between them into account. This certainly complicates the computational engine.

The situation is a bit easier in the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{FinVect}
of finite-dimensional vector spaces over a field, where every object is injective. The computational approach to cohomology, in which injective resolution of a given sheaf is built step by step, looks a bit scary. However, we notice here that our homology-slaying Algorithm
2
to be described in the latter sections, can be reformulated as the construction of a minimal injective resolution which is, in a sense, universal for all sheaves
D
on a given poset
S
.

C.3.
Roos complex

The definition of sheaf cohomology given in Subsection
C.2
will be used mainly for mathematical reference: it is universal, but seems impractical when it comes to actual calculations as shown in Remark
C.21
. There seems to be a consensus, both in theoretical and applied community, that when it comes to (co)homology computations, it is better to deal directly with (co)chain complexes, see Subsection
C.1
.

Given a diagram
D
on a poset
S
(equiv. a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on a finite topological space
X_{S}
) we want to define a cochain complex
(C^{*}(S;D),d)
which is more-or-less easily constructed from
D
, and computes cohomology of
D
, i.e.

H^{j}(C^{*}(S;D),d)\cong H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D}).

For any finite poset
S
there is a classical construction introduced in the paper of Roos
[
171
]
.

Construction C.22
.

Let
\tau
be a chain
s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{j}
in the poset
S
, which means that
\tau
is a
j
-dimensional simplex of the order complex
\ord(S)
. Denote by
\tau_{\max}
the largest element
s_{k}
of this chain. If
\tau\subseteq\sigma
is a set-theoretic inclusion of chains, then clearly
\tau_{\max}\leq\sigma_{\max}
.

Construction C.23
.

Assume that
\sigma
is a
(j+1)
-dimensional simplex of
\ord(S)
, i.e. a chain
s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{j+1}
, and
\tau
is a
j
-dimensional simplex. Then we can define the incidence number
[\sigma\colon\tau]\in\mathbb{Z}
in the standard alternating fashion. If
\sigma
is a chain
s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{j+1}
and
\tau=\sigma\setminus\{s_{i}\}
, then
[\sigma\colon\tau]
is set equal to
(-1)^{i}
; in all other cases
[\sigma\colon\tau]
is set equal to
0
. This alternating assumption easily implies that, for any
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}
,
\dim\sigma_{1}=j-1
,
\dim\sigma_{2}=j+1
, we have

(C.15)

\sum_{\tau\colon\dim\tau=j}[\sigma_{2}\colon\tau]\cdot[\tau\colon\sigma_{1}]=0.

There are only two nonzero summands in this sum.

Definition C.24
.

Let
D
be a diagram on a poset
S
. Consider the graded vector space
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
, and a map
d_{Roos})
where

C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\ord(S),\dim\tau=j}D(\tau_{\max}),\mbox{ and }d_{Roos}\colon C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)\to C^{j+1}_{Roos}(S;D)

defined by

d=\bigoplus_{\tau<\sigma,\dim\tau=j,\dim\sigma=j+1}[\sigma\colon\tau]F(\tau_{\max}\leq\sigma_{\max}).

It follows from (
C.15
) that
d^{2}=0
. The cochain complex
(C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D),d_{Roos})
is called
the Roos complex
of the diagram
D
on
S
, and its cohomology are called
the Roos cohomology
:

H^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)=H^{j}(C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D),d_{Roos}).

Remark C.25
.

Given a diagram
D
on
S
, we can naturally define a refined commutative diagram
\hat{D}\colon\mathbf{cat}(\ord(S))\to\mathbf{V}
, given by
\hat{D}(\sigma)=D(\sigma_{\max})
and
\hat{D}(\sigma\subset\tau)=D(\sigma_{\max}\leq\tau_{\max})
. Roos complex is basically the cellular cochain complex associated with the cellular sheaf (local coefficient system)
\hat{D}
on
\ord(S)
. However, since cellular sheaves and cellular cochain complex haven’t been defined yet, we avoid putting this remark as the definition of Roos complex to avoid any accusation in looping the definitions.

Construction C.26
.

Let
T\subseteq S
be a lower order ideal of
S
, i.e. a set with the property that
s\in T
and
s^{\prime}<s
implies
s^{\prime}\in T
. Equivalently,
T
is a closed subset of the Alexandrov topological space
X_{S}
. Let
V\in\mathbf{V}
be an object of the abelian category. Consider the diagram
V_{T}\in\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
defined by

V_{T}(s)=\begin{cases}V,&\mbox{if }s\in T\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\mbox{ and }V_{T}(s^{\prime}\leq s)=\begin{cases}\id_{V},&\mbox{if }s\in T\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We denote the corresponding sheaf on
X_{S}
with the same letter
V_{T}
.

The following statement shows the topological nature of the Roos construction.

Proposition C.27
.

If
T\subseteq S
is a lower order ideal, then Roos cohomology
H^{*}_{Roos}(X_{S};V_{T})
is naturally isomorphic to the singular cohomology
H^{*}(|T|;V)
of the geometrical realization.

Proof.

The Roos complex
(C^{j}_{Roos}(S;V_{T}),d_{Roos})
by construction coincides with the simplicial cochain complex of the simplicial complex
\ord(T)
with coefficients in
V
, hence
H^{*}_{Roos}(S;V_{T})\cong H^{*}_{simp}(|T|;V)
. The latter modules are isomorphic to simplicial, and hence singular cohomology of the geometrical realization, which is a standard fact in topology
[
105
, Thm.2.27]
. ∎

Example C.28
.

The basic injective sheaf
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
defined in Construction
C.8
coincides with
W_{S_{\leq s}}
. We have
H^{*}_{Roos}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})\cong H^{*}(|S_{\leq s}|;W)
. The latter cohomology is trivial in positive degrees and isomorphic to
W
in degree
0
, as follows from contractibility of
|S_{\leq s}|
.

A classical theorem asserts that Roos cohomology of a sheaf are isomorphic to cohomology defined from the injective resolution.

Theorem C.29
.

For any sheaf
D
on a poset
S
, the Roos cohomology modules are naturally isomorphic to its cohomology:

H^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)=H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D}).

Basically, this statement is the instance of the universal delta-functor theorem. We postpone the proof to Subsection
C.6
, where it is formulated in bigger generality. The next claim follows from Theorem
C.29
and Proposition
C.27
.

Remark C.30
.

Theorem
C.29
implies
H^{0}_{Roos}(S;D)\cong\Gamma(S;D)
. This particular statement can be seen directly: it is a simple exercise to prove that, for the Roos differential
d_{Roos}\colon C^{0}_{Roos}(S;D)\to C^{1}_{Roos}(S;D)
, the equation
d_{Roos}x=0
coincides with coherence equations
2.2
.

A number of important corollaries follow from Theorem
C.29
.

Corollary C.31
.

If
T\subseteq S
is a lower order ideal, then the sheaf cohomology
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};V_{T})
is naturally isomorphic to the singular cohomology
H^{*}(|T|;V)
of the geometrical realization.

Example C.32
.

When
T=S
, the sheaf
V_{S}
is just the constant sheaf on
S
(see Construction
2.22
), we denoted it by
\bar{V}
. From the Corollary
C.31
, it follows that
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};\bar{V})\cong H^{*}(|X|;V)
; it is nice to have all notions consistent throughout mathematics. In particular, it follows that
\Gamma(X_{S};\overline{\Bbbk^{1}})\cong H^{0}(|S|;\Bbbk)\cong\Bbbk^{c(|S|)}
, where
c(|S|)
is the number of connected components of the geometrical realization
|S|
, the latter property is well known for singular (co)homology.

Another important Corollary of Theorem
C.29
states that, for a finite poset, cohomology in sufficiently large degrees vanish.

Corollary C.33
.

Let
h=\dim\ord S
be the length of the longest chain in
S
. Then, for any sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on
X_{S}
we have
H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})=0
for
j>h
.

Proof.

By definition, the graded components
C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)
of the Roos complex vanish for
j>h
: there are no chains of length
>h
in
S
. Therefore cohomology
H^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)
vanish as well, and the rest follows from Theorem
C.29
.
∎

We finish this section with a general remark.

Remark C.34
.

The alternative way to reduce cohomology computation to a simplicial structure is provided by the notion of
Čech cohomology
. In this approach, an arbitrary topological space is replaced with the nerve of its covering, and the computation is performed on this simplicial complex. We do not give a review of the related theory here. However, it should be mentioned that Čech cohomology of sheaves on finite posets are not necessarily isomorphic to the cohomology
H^{*}_{AG}
as observed by Husainov
[
113
, Sect.5.4]
. The analogue of Theorem
C.29
does not hold, therefore one should be careful about which type of cohomology is used.

C.4.
Algorithmic issues

Definition C.35
.

Assume that an abelian category
\mathbf{V}
has finite direct sums. We say that
\mathbf{V}
is
homologically feasible
if, given a triple
V_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}V_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}V_{1}
in
\mathbf{V}
satisfying
d_{0};d_{1}=0
, the problem of describing subquotients
H=\Ker d_{1}/\im d_{0}
is algorithmically solvable.

Example C.36
.

The category
\Bbbk\mathbf{FinVect}
of finite dimensional vector spaces is homologically feasible — as long as arithmetical properties of the ground field
\Bbbk
are described somehow. This follows from the Gauss algorithm.

Example C.37
.

The category
\mathbf{FinAbel}
of finitely generated abelian groups is homologically feasible as well since the classification of finitely generated abelian groups is constructive.

Example C.38
.

The category
\Bbbk[t]\mathbf{FinMod}
of finitely generated modules over a polynomial ring in a single variable is homologically feasible by the same reason. The ring
\Bbbk[t]
is a principal ideal domain, and the classification of finitely generated modules over a PiD is constructive. The latter example is of particular importance in topological data analysis, since persistence modules, in particular persistent homology, are the objects of the category of (graded)
\Bbbk[t]
-modules.

The fact that cohomology of Roos complex coincides with cohomology defined in derived manner, has an important corollary.

Corollary C.39
.

Assume
X
is a finite topology, and
\mathbf{V}
is a homologically feasible category. Then, for any sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on
X
valued in
\mathbf{V}
, all the cohomology
H^{*}_{AG}(X;\mathcal{D})
are computable.

Proof.

Proposition
A.7
tells that
X=X_{S}
for some preposet
S
, and according to Remark
B.3
, without loss of generality,
S
can be assumed a poset. For a poset
S
, Theorem
C.29
claims that
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})\cong H^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
. Roos cohomology are computed as subquotients of the Roos complex
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
. Since there are only finitely many chains in a finite poset
S
, the whole complex
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
is a finite direct sum of stalks. For sufficiently large
j
(for example for
j>\#S
), the component
C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)
simply vanishes. The assumption of algorithmic feasibility implies that cohomology are computable in all degrees
j
below
\#S
.
∎

C.5.
Cohomology of cellular sheaves

Cohomology computation with the use of Roos complex is universal — it works for all sheaves on Alexandrov spaces. In the case of a finite poset and a diagram valued in finite-dimensional vector spaces, the computation of cohomology can be done algorithmically, as Corollary
C.39
shows. However, such computation is heavy in practice: the order complex
\ord S
of a general poset
S
has many simplices, so the matrices involved in the calculation of Roos cohomology are exponential in the cardinality of
S
, in the worst case.

Another approach to calculate cohomology of a sheaf is more optimal but it narrows the class of posets on which it applies. In this section we review the construction and basic theory of cellular cohomology which are well defined on cell posets, or their analogues, given by Definitions
A.26
,
A.30
, and
A.31
.The mathematical results of this subsection were proved in
[
76
]
, and the definition of a cellular cochain complex appears in all applied papers dealing with cohomology of cellular sheaves. We provide formulations in terms introduced previously in the paper.

Definition C.40
.

A
cellular sheaf

D
is a diagram on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
. Equivalently, a cellular sheaf is a sheaf on the Alexandrov space corresponding to a cell poset.

The property of “being cellular” is actually not the property of a sheaf itself, but rather the property of its domain of definition.

Remark C.41
.

The previous sections, especially Proposition
B.9
should have convinced the reader that there is not much operational difference between posets
S
and Alexandrov topologies
X_{S}
when we speak of diagrams/sheaves. To avoid the notational monster
X_{\mathcal{X}}
, we abuse the notation and denote by
\mathcal{X}
both cell posets (or the variations of this notion) and the corresponding Alexandrov topologies.

In order to define a cochain complex, which computes sheaf cohomology of cellular sheaves in a more optimal way than Roos complex, one needs an important ingredient: incidence numbers
25
25

25

At this place the perfect symphony of abstract nonsense is first broken by a down-to-earth notion of a number. The harmony will be restored later on in the text, where we show that incidence numbers are homology as well.
of cells. This notion is classical in the study of CW-complexes in algebraic topology.

Let
\sigma
be a cell of rank
k
of a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
. By definition, it means that the space
|\partial C(\sigma)|=|\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma}|
has integral homology of a
(k-1)
-dimensional sphere. From the contractibility of the cone
|C(\sigma)|
and the long exact sequence of the pair
(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|)
, it follows that

H^{k}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}

while all other relative homology groups vanish:
H^{j}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})=0
for
j\neq\rk\sigma
.

Definition C.42
.

A choice of a generator
\kappa_{\sigma}
of the group
H^{k}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is called
an orientation
of a cell
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
.

Construction C.43
.

Assume that
\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{X}
be two cells of a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
such that
\tau<\sigma
and
\rk\tau=k
,
\rk\sigma=k+1
for some
k=0,1,\ldots
. Consider the map
f_{\sigma,\tau}
given by composing the sequence

(C.16)

H_{k+1}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{H}_{k}(|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(|\partial C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)\setminus\{\tau\}|;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{k}(|C(\tau)|,|\partial C(\tau)|;\mathbb{Z}),

where

(1)

The first map
f_{1}\colon H_{k+1}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})
is the connecting homomorphism in the long exact sequence of singular homology of the pair
(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|)
. This map is an isomorphism since the cone
|C(\sigma)|
is contractible.

(2)

The second map
H_{k}(|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(|\partial C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)\setminus\{\tau\}|;\mathbb{Z})
is the natural map to relative homology.

(3)

The last isomorphism is due to excision property of singular homology.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is a cell complex, both groups
H_{k+1}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{k}(|C(\tau)|,|\partial C(\tau)|;\mathbb{Z})
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, so the composition of (
C.16
) belongs to
\Hom(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. If the orientations
\kappa_{\sigma}
and
\kappa_{\tau}
of cells
\sigma
and
\tau
are fixed, the map
f_{\sigma,\tau}
becomes a number as well.

Definition C.44
.

Let
\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{X}
be two cells of a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
such that
\rk\sigma=\rk\tau+1
, and let
\kappa_{\sigma}
and
\kappa_{\tau}
be orientations of these cells.
The incidence number

[\sigma\colon\tau]\in\mathbb{Z}
is defined as the unique integer such that

f_{\sigma,\tau}(\kappa_{\sigma})=[\sigma\colon\tau]\kappa_{\tau},

— in the case
\tau<\sigma
. If
\sigma\ngtr\tau
, we set
[\sigma\colon\tau]=0
.

The following statement is the classical property of incidence numbers, which allows to use them in the definition of a differential in a cellular cochain complex.

Lemma C.45

(Diamond property)
.

Let
\sigma,\tau
be two cells of a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
such that
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}
and
\rk\sigma_{2}=\rk\sigma_{1}+2
. Then

(C.17)

\sum_{\tau\colon\sigma_{1}<\tau<\sigma_{2}}[\sigma_{2}\colon\tau]\cdot[\tau\colon\sigma_{1}]=0.

Proof.

The classical sketch of proof. Let
\rk\sigma_{2}=k+1
, so that
\rk\sigma_{1}=k-1
, and consequently
\rk\tau=k
. Since
[\sigma\colon\tau]=0
when
\tau
is incomparable with
\sigma
, we can replace the required formula (
C.17
) by the equivalent formula

(C.18)

\sum_{\tau\colon\rk\tau=k}[\sigma_{2}\colon\tau]\cdot[\tau\colon\sigma_{1}]=0.

The direct sum
\bigoplus_{\sigma\colon\rk\sigma=k+1}H_{k+1}(|C(\sigma)|,|\partial C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})
is naturally identified with
H_{k+1}(|\mathcal{X}_{k+1}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k}|;\mathbb{Z})
(recall the definition of a skeleton in Construction
A.28
). The direct sum of all maps
f_{\sigma,\tau}
over all
\sigma
and
\tau
of ranks
k+1
and
k
respectively becomes identified with the map
\delta_{k+1}\colon H_{k+1}(|\mathcal{X}_{k+1}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k}|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(|\mathcal{X}_{k}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k-1}|;\mathbb{Z})
, which is the connecting homomorphism in the long exact sequence of the triple
(|\mathcal{X}_{k+1}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k-1}|)
. The choice of orientations of the cells defines the basis of both domain and target abelian group, therefore the map
\delta_{k+1}
becomes an integral matrix
D_{k+1}
with entries
[\sigma\colon\tau]
.

Equality (
C.18
) is equivalent to
D_{k}D_{k+1}=0
. This directly follows from the fact that two connecting homomorphisms
\delta_{k+1}\colon H_{k+1}(|\mathcal{X}_{k+1}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k}|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(|\mathcal{X}_{k}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k-1}|;\mathbb{Z})
and
\delta_{k}\colon H_{k}(|\mathcal{X}_{k}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k-1}|;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k-1}(|\mathcal{X}_{k-1}|,|\mathcal{X}_{k-2}|;\mathbb{Z})
compose to zero. Indeed, these connecting homomorphisms are nothing but the 1-st differential
d_{1}
of the spectral sequence in homology, associated with the filtration

\varnothing\subset|\mathcal{X}_{0}|\subset|\mathcal{X}_{1}|\subset|\mathcal{X}_{2}|\subset\cdots,

therefore
d_{1}^{2}=0
is trivially satisfied.
∎

Less involved proofs of the stated fact may be found in any basic book on algebraic topology, when the notion of cell cohomology is introduced, see e.g.
[
105
, p.139]
.

Definition C.46
.

For a cellular sheaf
D
on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
, consider the cochain complex
(C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D),d_{CW})

C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\mathcal{X},\rk\sigma=j}D(s),\qquad d_{CW}\colon C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)\to C^{j+1}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D),

where
d_{CW}=\bigoplus_{\tau<\sigma,\rk\tau=j,\rk\sigma=j+1}[\sigma\colon\tau]F(\tau<\sigma)
. This cochain complex is called
the cellular cochain complex
of the cellular sheaf
D
. The cohomology of
(C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D),d_{CW})
are called
cellular cohomology
of a cellular sheaf
D
and are denoted

H^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)=H^{j}(C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D),d_{CW}).

Remark C.47
.

The most nontrivial part of this definition is the property
d_{CW}^{2}=0
. It is easily proven from the diamond property of incidence numbers and commutativity of a diagram
D
itself. Indeed, if we consider the block of the map
d_{CW}^{2}
acting from
D(\sigma_{1})\subset C^{k-1}(\mathcal{X};D)
to
D(\sigma_{2})\subset C^{k+1}(\mathcal{X};D)
(where
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}
), this block equals
D(\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2})
multiplied by
\sum_{\tau\colon\sigma_{1}<\tau<\sigma_{2}}[\sigma_{2}\colon\tau]\cdot[\tau\colon\sigma_{1}]
since we summate the compositions of maps over all saturated paths leading from
\sigma_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
. The latter sum vanishes due to the diamond property, Lemma
C.45
.

Theorem C.48
.

For any cellular sheaf
D
on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
, the cellular cohomology modules are naturally isomorphic to its cohomology:

H^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)=H^{j}_{AG}(\mathcal{X};D).

We prove both Theorems
C.29
and
C.48
with the same general argument in subsection
C.6
. The next remark explains, why Theorem
C.48
is important even if we don’t care about higher order cohomology.

Remark C.49
.

As stated in Proposition
C.17
, we have
H^{0}_{AG}(\mathcal{X};D)\cong\Gamma(\mathcal{X};D)
, the space of global sections. In the original formulation, the set
\Gamma(\mathcal{X};D)
is defined as the solution to the system of compatibility equations, see Definition
2.17
or Remark
B.17
. However, Theorem
C.48
implies that

H^{0}_{AG}(\mathcal{X};D)\cong H^{0}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)=\Ker d_{CW}\colon C^{0}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)\to C^{1}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D).

The number of equations in the system
d_{CW}x=0
is less than the number of all compatibility equations (even restricted to the edges of the Hasse diagram of a poset, as in Remark
2.19
), because it only involves relations between vertices and edges, with no higher-dimensional cells involved. Informally this means that, in a cell complex, higher-dimensional cells do not store any compatibility information — it all lives in the 1-skeleton
\mathcal{X}_{1}
. This phenomenon reflects the cellular approximation theorem
[
105
, p.349]
, known in algebraic topology: any “proof of coherence” is a path through a space, and this path can be deformed to a path lying in the 1-skeleton. The same remark holds true for cellular complexes even if a sheaf takes values in non-abelian category, such as
\mathbf{Sets}
: we don’t need all compatibility equations (
2.2
) to describe global sections, — equations between 0- and 1-dimensional cells do the job.

C.6.
Honest cohomology computations

Let us fix a general poset
S
and an abelian category
\mathbf{V}
. Consider an additive functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
endowed with a natural transformation
\Gamma(X_{S};\cdot)\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\cdot)^{0}
which will be called
an augmentation
. The latter means that, for any sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on
X_{S}
we have natural maps from the global sections
\mathcal{D}(S)=\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}D
to the 0-degree component
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})^{0}
of the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
.

Definition C.50
.

We say that
\mathfrak{F}

honestly computes sheaf cohomology
, if
H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D}))
is naturally isomorphic to
H_{AG}^{*}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})
.

Recall that
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
is a basic injective sheaf on
S
defined in Construction
C.8
. The next theorem is a reformulation of Grothendieck’s delta-functor theorem
[
96
, Thm.2.2.2]
which we find more convenient to use in practice.

Theorem C.51
.

Assume that the functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
is exact and for every injective object
W\in\mathbf{V}
we have
H^{j}(\mathfrak{F}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))=0
if
j\neq 0
and the augmentation functor induces the natural isomorphism
W=\Gamma(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})\to H^{0}(\mathfrak{F}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))
. Then
\mathfrak{F}
honestly computes sheaf cohomology.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a sheaf on
X_{S}
(
D
a diagram on
S
). Consider an injective resolution (
C.11
) of
\mathcal{D}
:

0\to\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{I}_{0}\to\mathcal{I}_{1}\to\cdots

where each
\mathcal{I}_{j}
is a direct sum of injectives of the form
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
. Applying the functor
\mathfrak{F}
to this injective resolution we get a bicomplex

(C.19)

\begin{CD}00\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\mathcal{I}_{0}(S)@>{}>{}>\mathcal{I}_{1}(S)@>{}>{}>\cdots\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})^{0}@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{I}_{0})^{0}@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{I}_{1})^{0}@>{}>{}>\cdots\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})^{1}@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{I}_{0})^{1}@>{}>{}>\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{I}_{1})^{1}@>{}>{}>\cdots\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\vdots\vdots\vdots\ddots\end{CD}

Here we added the augmentation map on top of each column except the leftmost one.

In the first nontrivial row of (
C.19
), the global sections of the injective resolution of
\mathcal{D}
are written. The cohomology of this complex is, by definition, the sheaf cohomology
H_{AG}^{*}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})
. All other rows are acyclic, since we assumed the functor
\mathfrak{F}
is exact.

In the first nontrivial column of (
C.19
) the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
itself is written. All other columns are acyclic, because the augmented complex
0\to\mathcal{I}(S)\to\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{I})^{*}
is assumed acyclic for any injective of the form
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
, — hence for any direct sum of such sheaves.

Now, the standard argument of homological algebra (see e.g.
[
150
, Theorem 2.15]
) shows that cohomology of the first row is naturally isomorphic to the cohomology of the first column:
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})\cong H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D}))
, which was required.
∎

Remark C.52
.

Notice that the statement somehow converse to Theorem
C.51
holds true: if
\mathfrak{F}
honestly computes cohomology, then for any injective sheaf
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
, the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})
is acyclic in degrees
j>0
, while
H^{0}(\mathfrak{F}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))\cong W
. Indeed, each injective sheaf is acyclic, see Remark
C.20
, so this statement is trivial.

The following are the two main classes of examples of honest cohomology computation.

Example C.53
.

Let
D
be a diagram on a poset
S
. Roos complex
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
is defined, see Definition
C.24
. This construction defines a functor
\mathfrak{F}_{Roos}\colon\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
— the functoriality is easily seen from the definition. It is an exact functor, because each module
C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)
is a direct sum of stalks
D(s)
of a sheaf, and taking a stalk is an exact functor according to Proposition
C.6
. The augmentation map
\mathcal{D}(S)\to C^{0}_{Roos}(S;D)
is naturally defined since
C^{0}_{Roos}(S;D)
is a direct sum of stalks, and
\mathcal{D}(S)
in the inverse limit
\lim\limits_{\leftarrow S}D
of a diagram, hence maps naturally to all its stalks.

In order to apply Theorem
C.51
, we need to check that, for any injective
W\in\mathbf{V}
, the augmented complex
0\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}(X_{S})\to C^{*}_{Roos}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})
is acyclic. This is indeed the case, see Example
C.28
. The assumptions of Theorem
C.51
are fulfilled for the Roos functor
\mathfrak{F}_{Roos}
, hence
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};\mathcal{D})\cong H_{Roos}^{*}(S;D)
. This proves Theorem
C.29
.

Example C.54
.

Let
D
be a cellular sheaf on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
. Cellular cochain complex
C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
is defined, see
C.46
. This construction defines a functor
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}\colon\mathbf{Diag}(\mathcal{X};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
— the functoriality is easily seen from the definition. It is an exact functor, because each module
C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
is a direct sum of stalks
D(s)
of a sheaf, and taking a stalk is an exact functor. The augmentation map
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\to C^{0}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
is naturally defined since
C^{0}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
is a direct sum of stalks, and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})
in the inverse limit
\lim\limits_{\leftarrow\mathcal{X}}D
of a diagram, hence maps to all its stalks.

Let us check that, for any injective
W\in\mathbf{V}
, the augmented complex
0\to{{}_{\lceil}\sigma_{\rceil W}}(\mathcal{X})\to C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};{{}_{\lceil}\sigma_{\rceil W}})
is acyclic. The augmented cellular cochain complex takes the form

(C.20)

0\to W\to\bigoplus_{\tau\leq\sigma,\rk\tau=0}W\to\bigoplus_{\tau\leq\sigma,\rk\tau=1}W\to\bigoplus_{\tau\leq\sigma,\rk\tau=2}W\to\cdots

Recall that precise definition of the cellular differential required the auxiliary notion of incidence numbers, which came from topological considerations. It is time to dig these considerations up. Notice that the differential complex (
C.20
) is by construction the result of application of a functor
\Hom(\cdot,W)
to the augmented chain complex

(C.21)

0\leftarrow W\leftarrow C_{0}^{CW}(|C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow C_{1}^{CW}(|C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow C_{2}^{CW}(|C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow\cdots

where each
C_{j}^{CW}(|C(\sigma)|;\mathbb{Z})
is by definition the relative homology group
C_{j}(|C(\sigma)_{j}|,|C(\sigma)_{j-1}|;\mathbb{Z})
. The homological spectral sequence of the skeletal filtration
\varnothing\subset|C(\sigma)_{0}|\subset|C(\sigma)_{1}|\subset\cdots
degenerates at
E^{2}
-page by the vanishing argument. Therefore the homology of the complex (
C.21
) coincides with the reduced (singular) homology of the space
|C(\sigma)|
itself. Since
|C(\sigma)|
is contractible, its reduced homology vanish. Therefore, the cohomology of the cochain complex (
C.20
) vanish as well by the universal coefficients’ theorem for cohomology.

The assumptions of Theorem
C.51
are fulfilled for the CW-cochain functor
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}
. Therefore
H^{*}_{AG}(\mathcal{X};D)\cong H_{CW}^{*}(\mathcal{X};D)
which proves Theorem
C.48
.

Next example is very particular, but it illustrates that the general technique which proves Theorem
C.51
works pretty well on more concrete examples, which are not covered by Examples
C.53
and
C.54
.

Example C.55
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a poset described in Example
A.34
. It is a Morse cell poset with 3 vertices
1,2,3
and two edges
a,b
, with the edge
a
attached to vertices
1
and
2
, and the edge
b
attached to the middle of
a
and the vertex
3
. For every sheaf
D
on
\mathcal{Y}
consider the augmented cochain complex

0\to\lim\limits_{\leftarrow\mathcal{Y}}D\to C^{0}(\mathcal{Y};D)\to C^{1}(\mathcal{Y};D)\to 0

where

C^{0}(\mathcal{Y};D)=D(1)\oplus D(2)\oplus D(3),\qquad C^{1}(\mathcal{Y};D)=D(a)\oplus D(b),

the augmentation is a natural map from the inverse limit, and the differential
d\colon C^{0}(\mathcal{Y};D)\to C^{1}(\mathcal{Y};D)
is defined by the block matrix

(C.22)

\begin{pmatrix}D(1<a)&-D(2<a)&0\\
0&D(2<b)&-D(3<b)\end{pmatrix}.

Then the functor
\mathfrak{F}=C^{*}(\mathcal{Y};\cdot)
honestly computes sheaf cohomology on
\mathcal{Y}
. Indeed, all the sheaves
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
are
\mathfrak{F}
-acyclic. The most interesting case is
s=b
: in this case acyclicity of
{{}_{\lceil}b_{\rceil W}}
can be proven by hand.

Informally, the matrix (
C.22
) corresponds to first taking “CW-approximation” in the spirit of Whitehead theorem (see Fig.
8
), and considering incidence numbers of the resulting cellular replacement. Notice however, that on the level of homological algebra, there is no need to take an actual cellular approximation. Instead of matrix (
C.22
) one can define the differential to be

(C.23)

\begin{pmatrix}D(1<a)&-D(2<a)&0\\
k_{1}D(2<b)&k_{2}D(2<b)&-D(3<b)\end{pmatrix}

with
k_{1}+k_{2}=1
, and this cochain complex still honestly computes sheaf cohomology on a given poset
\mathcal{Y}
. This procedure can be considered “a fuzzy” CW-approximation.

The idea behind this example becomes more transparent in the next subsection, where we deal with general Morse posets.

C.7.
One-shot cohomology computations

There is a certain similarity shared by Examples
C.53
,
C.54
, and
C.55
. In all cases the graded components of the complex
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
, the one which honestly computes cohomology of a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
, are direct sums of certain stalks
D(s)
of
\mathcal{D}
. This motivates the following generalization.

Definition C.56
.

A functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
is called
concrete
, if for any
j\geqslant 0
, the graded component
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})_{j}
is a direct sum of stalks
D(s)
of a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
.

Each concrete functor is exact, since taking stalks is exact. Also there exists a natural augmentation map
\mathcal{D}(S)\to\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})_{0}
, because
\mathcal{D}(S)=\lim\limits_{\leftarrow}D
maps to each stalk by the universal property of the inverse limit.

However, it is important that in order for the functor to land in
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
one needs to define differentials in a uniform manner. We have already seen in Example
C.54
that this definition may be tricky: in the case of cell posets “to define differential in a uniform manner” reads as “to construct incidence numbers of cells”. This particular task is outsourced to basic algebraic topology books.

The cellular cochain complex
C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
is much smaller than Roos complex
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
, hence more usable in practice. One can summarize this optimality by the following two observations. In the cellular case, each stalk
D(\sigma)
appears in
C^{*}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
exactly once. In the Roos case, each stalk
D(s)
appears in
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
multiple times. The total number of summands in
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
is in the worst case exponential in the cardinality
|S|
. See more precise computations in Examples
C.71
and
C.73
below. This difference motivates the following definition.

Definition C.57
.

A concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
is said to be
one-shot
if each stalk
D(s)
of a sheaf
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbf{V})
appears exactly once as a summand in
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
.

Of course we are interested in one-shot concrete functors which provide honest cohomology calculations. We have already seen in Example
C.54
that cellular cochain complex provides one-shot honest cohomology calculation. On the other hand, Example
C.55
shows that non-cell poset may support a functor
\mathfrak{F}
that is both one-shot and honestly computes cohomology of sheaves.

Remark C.58
.

Is it possible to honestly compute cohomology with a concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}
without using some stalk
D(s)
at all? Somehow surprisingly, the answer is yes. Whenever
|S_{<s}|
is acyclic, the element
s\in S
can be dropped from the poset, and cohomology (of all sheaves) do not change, namely,
H^{j}(S;D)\cong H^{j}(S\setminus\{s\};D|_{S\setminus\{s\}})
. This can be seen as a particular case of the theorem of Oberst which holds in more general categorical setting; see
[
158
]
for derived direct limits of diagrams or
[
113
, Thm.3.10]
for the cohomological version, or the paper
[
115
, Prop.3.5]
where this result is reproved in the context of group homology and cohomology.

In the recent paper of Malko
[
149
]
this result is connected to beat-removals which were introduced in algebraic topology in the classical work of Stong
[
188
]
. The idea of beats’ removal recently found an application
[
31
]
: it can be used as a preliminary data simplification step before computing persistent homology, and it works impressively well on the data originating from ML applications. We believe, that a similar preprocessing can be applied for more general sheaf cohomology calculations. For example, if
s
is a downbeat, i.e. there is a unique edge of the Hasse diagram, which enters
s
, then
|S_{<s}|
is a cone, hence contractible, hence acyclic, hence
s
can be deleted without any effect on sheaf cohomology.

On the other hand, if
|S_{<s}|
is not acyclic, then it is impossible to not to use
D(s)
in the computations.

Construction C.59
.

Consider
the Dirac’s delta sheaf

\delta_{s}(W)
: the sheaf which has a nonzero value
W
concentrated in a single point
s
. It will be seen in the subsequent arguments, that whenever
|S_{<s}|
is not acyclic, the cohomology of
\delta_{s}(W)
are also nontrivial, hence cannot be honestly computed if
\delta_{s}(W)(s)=W
is not present in the cochain complex.

Next we formulate and prove a new result, which indicates the precise class of posets, suitable for one-shot honest cohomology computations.

Theorem C.60

(One-shot theorem)
.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be the category
