\mathbf{Abel}
of abelian groups or the category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
of vector spaces over arbitrary field. The following two conditions on a finite poset
S
are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a one-shot concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
, which honestly computes cohomology.

(2)

S
is a homology Morse cell poset (see Definition
A.32
).

We prove two implications separately. In both cases the arguments work in slightly bigger generality, and may be useful in their own right.

Proposition C.61
.

Assume that a concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
honestly computes cohomology of sheaves, and for an element
s\in S
, the stalk
D(s)
contributes exactly once in
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
, namely, for some
k\geqslant 0
, the stalk
D(s)
is a direct summand of the graded component
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})^{k}
. Then
|S_{<s}|
has the same homology as a sphere
S^{k-1}
.

Proof.

For convenience, we restrict considerations to the abelian category
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{Abel}
, the case
\mathbf{V}=\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
being similar, and even a bit easier. Let us denote the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})
by
C^{*}(S;D)
. Let
W\in\mathbf{V}
be an arbitrary injective abelian group. Consider two sheaves: the first one is
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}=W_{S_{\leq s}}
defined in Construction
C.8
, and another one is

(C.24)

{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}}=W_{S_{<s}}.

We have a short exact sequence of diagrams on
S
:

0\to\delta_{s}(W)\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}\to{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}}\to 0

where
\delta_{s}(W)
is the Dirac sheaf, whose stalk in
s
is
W
and zero otherwise. The functor
\mathfrak{F}
is concrete, hence exact, hence we have a short exact sequence of cochain complexes

(C.25)

0\to C^{*}(S;\delta_{s}(W))\to C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})\to C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}})\to 0.

By zig-zag Lemma (Lemma
C.4
), we have an induced long exact sequence in cohomology

\cdots\to H^{j}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))\to H^{j}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}}))\to H^{j+1}(C^{*}(S;\delta_{s}(W)))\to H^{j+1}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))\to\cdots

Since
\mathfrak{F}
honestly computes cohomology, the groups
H^{j}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))\cong H_{AG}^{j}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}})
vanish, since
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}
is injective (it doesn’t vanish in degree 0, where the map
H^{j}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))\to H^{j}(C^{*}(S;\delta_{s}(W)))
is an identity isomorphism
W\to W
, such cases should be considered separately, as usual). From this vanishing, we have an isomorphism
26
26

26

It can be considered a sheaf-theoretical analogue of the suspension isomorphism in singular homology.

(C.26)

H^{j+1}(C^{*}(S;\delta_{s}(W)))\cong H^{j}(C^{*}(S;{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}}))\mbox{ for }j>0.

Now notice that, by assumption, the stalk at
s
appears only once in the components of
\mathfrak{F}
of degree
k
, and
\delta_{s}(W)
is concentrated in the point
s
. Therefore
C^{k}(S;\delta_{s}(W))=W
, while other components of this cochain complex vanish. Passing to cohomology we get
H^{k}(S;\delta_{s}(W))=W
and all other cohomology groups vanish. Applying isomorphism (
C.26
) and remembering that
\mathfrak{F}
honestly computes sheaf cohomology we get that

(C.27)

H^{k-1}_{AG}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}})\cong W,\mbox{ and }H^{j}_{AG}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}})=0\mbox{ for }j\neq 0,k-1.

It remains to notice that
{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}}
is a sheaf supported on subposet
S_{<s}
which is a lower order ideal. Corollary
C.31
implies that
H^{*}_{AG}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil W}})
is isomorphic to singular cohomology of
|S_{<s}|
with coefficients in
W
. From the vanishing condition (
C.27
) it follows that

\tilde{H}^{j}(|S_{<s}|;W)=\begin{cases}W,&\mbox{if }j=k-1\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

for reduced cohomology groups of the geometrical realization. This shows that the space
|S_{<s}|
is a “
W
-cohomological
(k-1)
-sphere” for each injective group
W
. We need to show that this latter condition is the same as
|S_{<s}|
being a
\mathbb{Z}
-homological sphere. This last step is a bit technical, and can be omitted for vector spaces
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
.

Applying the universal coefficient theorem in cohomology
[
105
, p.195]
to the space
X=|S_{<s}|
we get the short exact sequence

0\to\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{j-1}(X;\mathbb{Z}),W)\to\tilde{H}^{j}(X;W)\to\Hom(\tilde{H}_{j}(X;\mathbb{Z});W)\to 0.

Since
W
is injective, the
\Ext
-term vanishes, so there is an isomorphism
\tilde{H}^{j}(X;W)\cong\Hom(\tilde{H}_{j}(X;\mathbb{Z});W)
. For
j\neq k-1
, the module
\tilde{H}^{j}(X;W)
vanishes for any
W
, therefore
\tilde{H}_{j}(X;\mathbb{Z})
vanishes as well (a nontrivial group can always be nontrivially mapped to its injective hull). For
j=k-1
, we have
\Hom(\tilde{H}_{j}(X;\mathbb{Z});W)\cong\tilde{H}^{j}(X;W)\cong W
for any injective
W
. In general there exist various abelian groups
G
satisfying the property

(C.28)

\Hom(G,W)\cong W\mbox{ for any injective }W,

not only
G=\mathbb{Z}
(for example
G=\mathbb{Q}
works as well). Luckily for us, the space
X=|S_{<s}|
is a finite simplicial complex, so its homology
\tilde{H}_{k}(X;\mathbb{Z})
with integral coefficients is a finitely generated abelian group. Among such groups, only
\mathbb{Z}
satisfies the property (
C.28
). This argument shows that
\tilde{H}_{*}(|S_{<s}|;\mathbb{Z})\cong\tilde{H}_{*}(S^{k-1};\mathbb{Z})
as required.
∎

Proposition
C.61
proves the implication
1\Rightarrow 2
in Theorem
C.60
. If every stalk
D(s)
is used once by an honest functor
\mathfrak{F}
, then every space
|S_{<s}|
has homology of a sphere, which is the definition of a homology Morse cell poset.

Remark C.62
.

Informally, the proof of implication
1\Rightarrow 2
in Theorem
C.60
can be characterized by the following mantra. “Let us treat stalks as bullets. If we want to shoot all cohomology with these bullets, we should be able in particular to shoot the subposets
S_{<s}
. To be able to do this in one shot, the total reduced homology of
S_{<s}
should be 1-dimensional, i.e.
|S_{<s}|
has homology of a sphere.”

Somehow surprisingly the proof of the opposite implication is a bit more subtle. This proof utilizes certain intuitions from the proof of Proposition
C.61
and eventually leads to a precise algorithm for cohomology computation for general posets to be described in the next subsection.

Construction C.63
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a homology Morse poset. If
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
is a cell, then there exists
k\geq 0
such that
|\partial C(\sigma)|=|\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma}|
has homology of
S^{k-1}
. In Subsection
A.7
we called such
k
the dimension of a cell
\sigma
. Notice that dimensions defined this way do not form a grading on a poset, they are not even monotonic: a situation may occur that
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}
while
\dim\sigma_{1}\geq\dim\sigma_{2}
as demonstrated in Example
A.34
. Nevertheless, we can try to construct a Morse chain complex
(C_{*}^{M}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z}),\partial)
which computes singular homology of
|\mathcal{X}|
in a geometrically meaningful way (to be made precise below). We set by definition

C_{j}^{M}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})=\bigoplus_{\dim\sigma=j}\mathbb{Z},

with
j
-dimensional cells
\sigma
forming the distinguished basis of this module. The nontrivial part of the construction is to define the chain differential
\partial\colon C_{j}^{M}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})\to C_{j-1}^{M}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})
, i.e. the incidence numbers
[\sigma\colon\tau]\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\partial(\sigma)=\sum_{\dim\tau=\dim\sigma-1}[\sigma\colon\tau]\tau
. We cannot just initialize incidence numbers at random if want to use them in honest cohomology calculations. They way how incidence numbers are used will tell us which properties they should satisfy.

Let’s pretend that we already have some incidence numbers. Then we can construct a functor
\mathfrak{F}_{M}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
similar to cellular cochain complex:

(C.29)

\mathfrak{F}_{M}(D)=C^{*}_{M}(\mathcal{X};D),\quad C^{*}_{M}(\mathcal{X};D)=\bigoplus_{\dim\sigma=j}D(\sigma),\quad d_{M}=\bigoplus_{\dim\tau=\dim\sigma-1}[\sigma\colon\tau]D(\tau<\sigma).

In order for
\mathfrak{F}_{M}
to be well-defined and honestly compute sheaf cohomology we need three conditions to be satisfied.

Requirement C.64
.

(1)

[\sigma\colon\tau]=0
if
\tau\nless\sigma
. Otherwise there are undefined summands in the differential
d_{M}
in formula (
C.29
).

(2)

\mathfrak{F}_{M}
should land in cochain complexes. This means
d_{M}^{2}=0
. Equivalently, incidence numbers should satisfy the diamond relation (
C.17
). Equivalently, we should have
\partial^{2}=0
, so
C^{M}_{*}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})
should be a chain complex.

(3)

The augmented complex
0\to I(\mathcal{X})\to\mathfrak{F}_{M}(I)
is acyclic for any injective sheaf
I
of the form
{{}_{\lceil}\sigma_{\rceil W}}
with injective group
W
. This latter condition is equivalent to vanishing of the reduced Morse homology of any subposet
C(\sigma)=\mathcal{X}_{\leq\sigma}
according to the universal coefficient theorem.

We will use the requirements
C.64
to define incidence numbers inductively in Construction
C.67
below. To justify the induction step we need a technical construction and a lemma, which resembles a topological analogue of Theorem
C.51
.

Construction C.65
.

Recall from Remark
A.35
that (Morse) cell subposet of a (Morse) cell poset
\mathcal{Y}
is any lower order ideal of
\mathcal{Y}
. Notice that whenever a Morse chain complex
(C_{*}^{M}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M})
is defined on
\mathcal{Y}
in the way that requirements
C.64
are fulfilled, it can be restricted, in a straightforward manner, to any cell subposet
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
. We denote the restricted chain complex by
(C^{M}_{*}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M})
— the incidence numbers are inherited from those on
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma C.66
.

Assume that incidence numbers are defined on a homology Morse poset
\mathcal{Y}
and satisfy the requirements
C.64
. Then, for any cell subposet
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
, the homology of the chain complex
(C^{M}_{*}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M})
are isomorphic to the integral homology of the geometrical realization of
\mathcal{Z}
:

H_{i}((C^{M}_{*}(\mathcal{Z},\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M}))\cong H_{i}(|\mathcal{Z}|;\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

We prove the lemma by clumsily reducing it to something already proven
27
27

27

Right in the spirit of anecdotes about mathematicians.
. Let
W
be any injective abelian group. Consider the sheaf
W_{\mathcal{Z}}
constantly supported on
\mathcal{Z}
as in Construction
C.26
. We have a sequence of isomorphisms

(C.30)

\begin{split}\Hom(H_{*}(|\mathcal{Z}|;\mathbb{Z}),W)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\cong}}H^{*}(|\mathcal{Z}|;W)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{\cong}}H^{*}_{AG}(\mathcal{Y};W_{Z})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{\cong}}H^{*}(C^{*}_{M}(\mathcal{Y};W_{\mathcal{Z}}))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{\cong}}H^{*}(\Hom((C_{*}^{M}(\mathcal{Z};\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M}),W))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 5}}{{\cong}}\Hom(H_{*}(C_{*}^{M}(\mathcal{Z};\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M}),W),\end{split}

where (1) is the universal coefficients isomorphism due to
W
being injective (hence the functor
\Hom(\cdot,W)
is exact), (2) is by Corollary
C.31
, (3) is by the fact that Morse complex functor
\mathfrak{F}_{M}
honestly computes cohomology (since incidence numbers on
\mathcal{Y}
are assumed to satisfy requirements
C.64
, we can apply Theorem
C.51
to this functor), (4) is just rewriting the definition of
C^{*}_{M}(\mathcal{Y};\mathcal{Z}_{W})
, and, finally, (5) is once again an exactness of
\Hom(\cdot,W)
for injective
W
.

If two groups
A,B
are finitely generated and satisfy
\Hom(A,W)\cong\Hom(B,W)
for any injective
W
, then
A\cong B
. Both groups
H_{*}(|\mathcal{Z}|;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{*}(C_{*}^{M}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M})
are finitely generated, hence the claim follows from (
C.30
).
∎

Now we are finally ready to construct incidence numbers for any finite Morse cell poset.

Construction C.67
.

At first, choose a topological sorting
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
of all cells of the Morse cell poset
\mathcal{X}
, i.e. a linear order in which, for any
\sigma_{i}<\sigma_{j}
we have
i<j
(all cells which are smaller than
\sigma_{j}
in the partial order, appear earlier in the list). Let
\mathcal{X}_{(j)}
denote the subposet
\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j}\}
of
\mathcal{X}
, with the induced partial order. We also formally set
\mathcal{X}_{(0)}=\varnothing
. All posets
\mathcal{X}_{(j)}
are Morse cell posets. Indeed,
(\mathcal{X}_{(j)})_{<\sigma}=\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma}=\partial C(\sigma)
by the defining property of a topological sorting. The poset
\mathcal{X}_{(j)}
is obtained from the poset
\mathcal{X}_{(j-1)}
by adding a cell
\sigma_{j}
. Topologically, the space
|\mathcal{X}_{(j)}|
is obtained from
|\mathcal{X}_{(j-1)}|
by attaching the cone
|C(\sigma_{j})|
over
\partial|C(\sigma_{j})|
with the apex in
\sigma_{j}
. We construct incidence numbers
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]
inductively for
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. The base of induction
j=0
is superfluous: there are no cells in the empty poset, hence no need to construct incidence numbers.

Assume that incidence numbers
[\sigma_{i}\colon\tau]
are already defined for
i\leq j-1
in a way that requirements
C.64
are satisfied on the poset
\mathcal{X}_{(j-1)}
. We need to extend this definition to the next filtration term
\mathcal{X}_{(j)}
. This means that we need to define
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]
for the newly added cell
\sigma_{j}
. Let
k=\dim\sigma_{j}
. If
k=0
, there is no need to construct incidence numbers
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]
since there are no cells
\tau
of dimension
-1
in the poset. Henceforth, in the following we assume
k\geq 1
.

The first natural requirement tells that
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]=0
if
\tau\nless\sigma_{j}
. So far, we may restrict to only those cells
\tau
which satisfy
\tau<\sigma_{j}
and
\dim\tau=k-1
. Consider the subposet
\partial C(\sigma_{j})=\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma_{j}}
— it is a cell subposet of
\mathcal{X}_{j-1}
. Since incidence numbers are already constructed on
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}_{j-1}
, we are in position to apply Lemma
C.66
to the cell subposet
\partial C(\sigma_{j})
, which gives an isomorphism

H_{*}(C^{M}_{*}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z}),\partial_{M})\cong H_{*}(|\partial C(\sigma_{j})|;\mathbb{Z})

Now, by assumption, the space
|\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma_{j}}|
has the same integral homology as a sphere
S^{k-1}
, i.e. the homology are concentrated in degrees
0
and
k-1
. Let us augment the chain complex
C^{M}_{*}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})
with the term
\mathbb{Z}
and the map

\partial\colon C^{M}_{0}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})=\bigoplus_{\dim\sigma=0}\mathbb{Z}\to C^{M}_{-1}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z},

which sends all the chosen generators
\sigma
,
\dim\sigma=0
, to
1
. Then reduced homology
\tilde{H}_{*}
(i.e. the homology of the augmented complex
0\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}\leftarrow C^{M}_{*}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})
) are concentrated in a single degree
k-1
. We have

\mathbb{Z}\cong\tilde{H}_{k-1}(C^{M}_{\ast}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z}))=\dfrac{Z_{k-1}}{B_{k-1}}=\dfrac{\Ker\partial\colon C^{M}_{k-1}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})\to C^{M}_{k-2}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})}{\im\partial\colon C^{M}_{k}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})\to C^{M}_{k-1}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})}.

Take any chain
c\in Z_{k-1}
of the Morse chain complex which represents a generator of
\mathbb{Z}\cong\tilde{H}_{k-1}(C_{*}^{M}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z}))
. By construction,
c
is an element of

C^{M}_{k-1}(\partial C(\sigma_{j});\mathbb{Z})=\bigoplus_{\tau\colon\dim\tau=k-1,\tau<\sigma_{j}}\mathbb{Z}

hence

c=\sum\limits_{\tau\colon\dim\tau=k-1,\tau<\sigma_{j}}b_{\tau}\tau.

Finally, we set
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]=b_{\tau}
.

Lemma C.68
.

The incidence numbers on
\mathcal{X}_{(j)}
defined by Construction
C.67
satisfy the requirements
C.64
.

Proof.

We do not need to check anything for the incidence numbers
[\sigma_{i}\colon\tau]
with
i<j
, since these numbers were inherited from
\mathcal{X}_{(j-1)}
and satisfy the requirements
C.64
by inductive hypothesis. So we only need to check the properties for
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]
. The first requirement is satisfied by assumption: we have
[\sigma_{j}\colon\tau]=0
unless
\tau<\sigma_{j}
. The second requirement is also satisfied: by construction we have

\partial_{M}(\sigma_{j})=\sum\limits_{\tau\colon\dim\tau=k-1,\tau<\sigma_{j}}b_{\tau}\tau=c\in Z_{k-1}=\Ker\partial_{M},

hence
\partial_{M}^{2}(\sigma_{j})=0
. Finally, the third property is about the subposet
\mathcal{X}_{\leq\sigma}
being Morse-acyclic. It holds by construction: the Morse complex of the poset
C(\sigma)
differs from the Morse complex of
\partial C(\sigma)
in one term
\mathbb{Z}
of degree
k=\dim\sigma
. When passing to homology, this term kills the unique
(k-1)
-homology of
\partial C(\sigma)
(without any torsion left, since
[c]
was chosen to be a generator of
H_{k-1}(\partial C(\sigma);\mathbb{Z})
), — and dies itself.
∎

Lemma
C.68
completes the induction step in the construction of incidence numbers on a Morse cell poset
\mathcal{X}
. Using these incidence numbers, we define the functor
\mathfrak{F}_{M}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(\mathcal{X};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{V}
by (
C.29
). It is a concrete one-shot functor which honestly computes cohomology of sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
. The implication
2\Rightarrow 1
of Theorem
C.60
is proven.

Remark C.69
.

It is possible to consider diagrams over arbitrary finite categories: cohomology can be defined as right derived functors similar to the case of posets. In this generality, one-shot computations of sheaf cohomology can be extended to cellular categories as defined in
[
137
]
.

C.8.
Structure agnostic cohomology computations

One might wonder, what prevents us from extending Remark
C.58
(about redundant stalks) and Theorem
C.60
(about stalks used exactly once) to more general classes of posets? Literally nothing! We develop more general approach in this subsection and provide an algorithm for sheaf cohomology computations which satisfies two properties.

(1)

The algorithm is applicable to sheaves over all posets. Its complexity (see Definition
C.70
) is lower than complexity of Roos complex, and is proven to be the minimal possible.

(2)

On the class of homology Morse cell posets (in particular on cell posets) this algorithm becomes the classical algorithm of cohomology calculation via cellular (or Morse) cochain complex: in this case the algorithm computes incidence numbers.

The ideas of this subsection are similar to the idea of local homology sheaf applied in stratification learning
[
36
]
, although the ultimate goals are different. While stratification learning aims to build a convenient filtration on a geometrical structure by utilizing sheaf theory
28
28

28

As well as, implicitly Zeeman–McCrory spectral sequence
[
152
]
, our goal is to construct an optimized algorithm for general sheaf cohomology computation. Our considerations also have much in common with those of
[
52
]
, where similar arguments were applied to constant sheaves on posets.

Definition C.70
.

Assume that a concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
honestly computes cohomology of sheaves on
S
. The number of times the stalk
D(s)
appears as a direct summand in the
k
-th graded component
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D})^{k}
is called
the multiplicity of

s
, and is denoted
\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F})
. The total number

c(\mathfrak{F})=\sum_{s\in S,k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F})

is called
the complexity of the functor

\mathfrak{F}
.

Example C.71
.

For a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
and a cellular cochain functor
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(\mathcal{X};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
we have

\mu_{k}(\sigma,\mathfrak{F}_{CW})=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if }k=\dim\sigma\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

for any cell
\sigma
. Notice that, whenever
\dim\sigma=0
, we necessarily have
\mathcal{X}_{<\sigma}=\varnothing
, and formally
\tilde{H}_{-1}(\varnothing;\Bbbk)=\Bbbk
(this homology comes from the augmentation term). Therefore
0
-dimensional cells are not exceptions in this example. The complexity
c(\mathfrak{F}_{CW})
equals the number of cells of
\mathcal{X}
.

Example C.72
.

For a homology Morse poset
\mathcal{X}
and a Morse cochain functor
\mathfrak{F}_{M}
, similar to the previous example, we have
\mu_{k}(\sigma,\mathfrak{F}_{M})=1
if
\dim\sigma=k
, and
0
otherwise. The complexity
c(\mathfrak{F}_{M})
again equals the number of cells of
\mathcal{X}
. These are precisely all one-shot functors according to Theorem
C.60
.

Example C.73
.

For the Roos cochain functor
\mathfrak{F}_{Roos}
we have

\mu_{\mathfrak{F}_{Roos}}(s)=f_{k-1}(\ord(S_{<s})),

where
f_{j}(\mathcal{K})
denotes the number of
j
-dimensional simplices of a nonempty simplicial complex
\mathcal{K}
, while it is formally assumed that
f_{-1}(\mathcal{K})=1
for any simplicial complex
\mathcal{K}
, even for
\mathcal{K}=\varnothing
. This formula follows easily from the count of chains
s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}<s
of
S
which end in a given element
s
. The complexity
c(\mathfrak{F}_{Roos})
equals the number of simplices of
\ord(S)
.

To avoid complications with torsion, for the rest of this subsection we work with the target category
\mathbf{V}=\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
, the category of vector spaces over a fixed field
\Bbbk
.

Theorem C.74
.

For any functor
\mathfrak{F}
which honestly computes cohomology of sheaves on a poset
S
, and for any element
s\in S
, the multiplicity
\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F})
is at least the total reduced Betti number
\tilde{\beta}_{k-1}(|S_{<s}|)=\dim\tilde{H}_{k-1}(|S_{<s}|;\Bbbk)
of the down-set of
s
.

Proof.

The proof is completely similar to Proposition
C.61
. Consider a short exact sequence of sheaves on
S
:

(C.31)

0\to\delta_{s}(\Bbbk)\to{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil\Bbbk}}\to{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil\Bbbk}}\to 0,

where
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil\Bbbk}}
is the basic injective sheaf defined in Construction
C.8
,
{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil\Bbbk}}
is defined in (
C.24
), and
\delta_{s}(\Bbbk)
is the Dirac diagram supported on
s
, see Construction
C.59
. Notice that
\dim\mathfrak{F}(\delta_{s}(\Bbbk))^{k}=\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F})
— because stalks in all points except
s
vanish, and
\delta_{s}(\Bbbk)(s)=\Bbbk
contributes to the
k
-th graded component of the cochain complex
\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F})
many times by construction.

Taking long exact sequence of cohomology induced by the short exact sequence (
C.31
), and noticing that the sheaf
{{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil\Bbbk}}
is acyclic, we see that
H_{AG}^{*}(X_{S};\delta_{s}(\Bbbk))\cong\tilde{H}_{AG}^{*-1}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil\Bbbk}})
. However, by Corollary
C.31
we know that
\tilde{H}_{AG}^{*-1}(X_{S};{{}_{\lceil}\bar{s}_{\rceil\Bbbk}})
is isomorphic to the singular cohomology
\tilde{H}^{*-1}(|S_{<s}|;\Bbbk)
. On the other hand,
H_{AG}^{*}(X_{S};\delta_{s}(\Bbbk))\cong H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}(\delta_{s}(\Bbbk)))
, since
\mathfrak{F}
honestly computes cohomology. Since cohomology is a subquotient of the cochain complex, we have

\tilde{\beta}_{k-1}(|S_{<s}|)=\dim H^{k}(\mathfrak{F}(\delta_{s}(\Bbbk)))\leq\dim\mathfrak{F}(\delta_{s}(\Bbbk))^{k}=\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F}),

as needed.
∎

Remark C.75
.

It follows from the proof of Theorem
C.74
that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\mu_{k,\mathfrak{F}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\tilde{\beta}_{k-1}(|S_{<s}|)
, whenever the first sum makes sense. Indeed, the first expression is the Euler characteristic of the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}(\delta_{s}(\Bbbk))^{k}
, while the second one is the Euler characteristic of its cohomology. It is well-known that Euler characteristics of any cochain complex and that of its cohomology coincide (see e.g.
[
105
, Thm.2.44]
or
[
167
, Lm.5.1]
. Together with Theorem
C.74
, this remark allows to derive Proposition
C.61
in the category
\mathbf{V}=\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
.

The next statement asserts that the estimation given by
C.74
is exact. There exists a functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
which realizes the lower theoretical bound on multiplicity for all elements
s\in S
at once, hence on the total complexity.

Theorem C.76
.

For any finite poset
S
, there exists a concrete functor

\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X_{S};\mathbf{V})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})

which honestly computes cohomology and satisfies
\mu_{k}(s,\mathfrak{F}_{\min})=\tilde{\beta}_{k-1}(|S_{<s}|)
for any
s\in S
.

To prove this statement we provide an algorithm which constructs the required functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
given a finite poset
S
. First we give some specifications and definitions to simplify the implementation of this algorithm.

Construction C.77
.

The main step, given an arbitrary poset
S
, is to construct a chain complex
(C_{*}(S;\Bbbk),\partial)
which stores information on “incidence numbers” (more preciesly, their analogues when no assumption on topology of
|S_{<s}|
is posed).

To encode a (finite-dimensional) chain complex
(C_{*},d)
over a field
\Bbbk
we use the data structure
\ChCpx
which consists of

(1)

a finite set
A
, denoting a set of homogeneous generators of the total space
\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}C_{j}
;

(2)

a grading
\deg\colon A\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(3)

a map
M\colon A\times A\to\Bbbk
, which denotes the matrix of an operator
\partial
in the chosen basis
A
(first component stands for input=indicates the columns, the second stands for the output and indicates rows).

The contract on
\ChCpx
(assumed but not checked explicitly) is that (1) for each
a,c\in A
we have
\sum_{b\in A}M(a,b)M(b,c)=0
(i.e.
d^{2}=0
), (2) for each
a,b\in A
with
\deg(a)\neq\deg(b)+1
,
M(a,b)=0
(the differential lowers degree by
1
).

We also have the augmented version of this structure,
\AChCpx
, where the grading takes values in
\{-1\}\sqcup\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. The cochain complex
\CochCpx
can be defined by transposing the matrix
M
, i.e. inverting the order of arguments (the only difference from chain complexes is that cohomological differential raises degree by
1
).

Two auxiliary procedures will be used in the algorithm, whose contracts are described below.

(1)

A function HomologyBasis consumes
\ChCpx
(resp.,
\AChCpx
) and outputs representing chains of generators of its homology, i.e. an indexing set
H
together with the degree function
\deg\colon H\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
(resp.
\{-1\}\sqcup\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
), and the representative chains
RC\colon H\to(A\to\Bbbk)
, satisfying the condition that
\deg(a)=\deg(i)
for any nonzero coefficient
RC(i)(a)\neq 0
. The procedure HomologyBasis has standard realizations based on variations of Gauss algorithm, see
[
120
]
and the implementation available in
[
173
]
.

(2)

A function Restrict consumes an augmented chain complex
(C_{*},d)
, on a set
A
of generators, and a subset
B\subset A
, and outputs the restricted complex, that is simply
B\times B
-block of the matrix of the differential. We assume the restriction thus defined makes sense, i.e. the differential preserves the vector subspace spanned by
B
.

(3)

A function TopoSort consumes a poset
S
and outputs an ordered list
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
of its elements which is a linearization of
S
.

Returning back to the complex associated with a poset, a vector space
C_{*}(S;\Bbbk)
will have type
\AChCpx
and consist of the triple
(G^{a},\deg,I)
, where
G^{a}
is the set of generators and
I
is the matrix of “incidence numbers”. All generators from
G^{a}
except the augmentation term will be stitched to specific elements of
S
, so we fix a decomposition
G^{a}=\{\varnothing\}\sqcup G
, and
G=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}G_{s}
.

The set
G^{a}
of generators and the matrix
I
are constructed inductively. We first choose a topological sorting of
S
and proceed by computing and killing homology of all down-sets
|S_{<s}|
one by one, according to the following algorithm. The values of
I(a,b)
which are not explicitly specified are set equal to
0
. Whether this procedure is actually needed depends on the representation format of matrices. For example, if the matrices are represented in sparse format, this procedure is unnecessary. However, in the subsequent algorithm of sheaf cohomology computation, it will be more convenient to work with matrices represented as dense matrices, or double arrays.

Algorithm 1

Incidence matrix computation

0:

Poset
S
.

1:

procedure

S_{sorted}
=TopoSort(
S
)

2:

create
generator
\varnothing
with
\deg(\varnothing)=-1

3:

define

C=(\{\varnothing\},\deg,I)
of type
\AChCpx

4:

for
each
s
in
S_{sorted}

do

5:

define

G_{s}=\{\}

6:

define

S_{<s}=\{t\in S\mid t<s\}

7:

define

G|_{<s}=\{\varnothing\}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{t\in S_{<s}}G_{t}

8:

procedure

C|_{<s}
=Restrict(
C
,
G|_{<s}
)

9:

procedure

(H,\deg,RC)
=HomologyBasis(
C|_{<s}
)

10:

for
each homology generator
i
in
H

do

11:

create
generator
g
with
\deg(g)
equal to
\deg(i)+1

12:

append

g
to
G_{s}

13:

for
each
h
in
G

do

14:

set

I(h,g)
equal to
0

15:

if

h
lies in
G|_{<s}

then

16:

set

I(g,h)
equal to
RC(i)(h)

17:

else

18:

set

I(g,h)
equal to
0

19:

end

if

20:

end

for

21:

end

for

22:

append

G_{s}
to
G^{a}

23:

end

for

24:

output
set of generators
G^{a}
, matrix
I

Proof of Theorem
C.76
.

As long as the set of generators
G^{a}
and the incidence matrix
I
are computed by Algorithm
1
, they can be used to compute cohomology of any sheaf
D
on
S
. Since we have a decomposition
G^{a}=\{\varnothing\}\sqcup G
and
G=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}G_{s}
, it will be convenient to adopt a convention from dependent type theory: if
g\in G
, then
\pr_{S}(g)\in S
denotes the index of the corresponding component of the union, and
\pr_{G}(g)\in G_{\pr_{S}(g)}
denotes the corresponding element of this component.

Recall that
I(g,h)
is the element of the matrix
I
, lying in
g
-th column and
h
-th row. Mathematically, given a diagram
D
on
S
(a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on the corresponding Alexandrov topological space
X_{S}
) we define the cochain complex
(C^{*}_{\min}(S;D),d_{\min})
by the formulas

(C.32)

C^{j}_{\min}(S;D)=\bigoplus_{g\in G,\deg g=j}D(\pr_{S}(g)),\qquad d_{\min}^{j}\colon C^{j}_{\min}(S;D)\to C^{j+1}_{\min}(S;D)

(C.33)

d_{\min}^{j}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}\in G\end{subarray}{\deg(g_{1})=j,\deg(g_{2})=j+1}}I(g_{2},g_{1})D(\pr_{S}(g_{1})<\pr_{S}(g_{2})).

for any
j=0,1,\ldots
. We set
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}(D)=(C^{*}_{\min}(S;D),d_{\min})
. This construction satisfies the following properties.

(1)

The differential is well-defined since
[g_{2}\colon g_{1}]
may be nonzero only if
\pr_{S}(g_{1})<\pr_{S}(g_{2})
, as can be seen directly from Algorithm
1
.

(2)

The differential
d_{\min}
satisfies
d_{\min}^{2}=0
. The proof is similar to Construction
C.67
. Hence
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
lands in
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
.

(3)

Since all summands in (
C.32
) are functorial,
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
is actually a functor from
\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
to
\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbf{V})
.

(4)

Finally, we have
H^{*}(W\to\mathfrak{F}_{\min}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))=0
for any
s\in S
and any vector space
W=\Bbbk^{n}
(which is automatically injective). Indeed, we have
\tilde{H}_{*}(C_{*}(S_{\leq s};\Bbbk),\partial)=0
by construction of the incidence matrix in Algorithm
1
. Applying the exact functor
\Hom(\cdot,W)
we get

H^{*}(W\to\mathfrak{F}_{\min}({{}_{\lceil}s_{\rceil W}}))=H^{*}(\Hom(C_{*}(S_{\leq s};\Bbbk)\to\Bbbk,W))=\\
\Hom(H_{*}(C_{*}(S_{\leq s};\Bbbk)\to\Bbbk),W)=0.

The assumption of Theorem
C.51
are satisfied, therefore
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
honestly computes cohomology of sheaves on
S
. The Algorithm
1
can be backtracked and it is seen that the multiplicity

\mu_{j}(s,\mathfrak{F}_{\min})=\#G_{s}

of a stalk
D(s)
in the vector space
C^{j}_{\min}(S;D)
equals the rank
\dim\tilde{H}_{j}^{\prime}
where

\tilde{H}_{j}^{\prime}=\tilde{H}_{j}(C|_{<s}),

is the homology of the complex restricted to generators associated with elements of
S_{<s}
. Notice that
S_{<s}
is a subset of
S^{\prime}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s\}
, the interval of topological sorting which have already been constructed. By induction, the incidence numbers already defined on
S^{\prime}
give honest sheaf cohomology computation for sheaves on
S^{\prime}
. According to Corollary
C.31
(also see details in the proof of Lemma
C.68
), the singular homology of lower order ideals of
S^{\prime}
can be honestly computed with the same incidence numbers (the restricted chain complex). This is applied to the lower order ideal
S_{<s}\subset S^{\prime}
. Therefore, it is proven by induction, that

\tilde{H}_{j}(C|_{<s})\cong\tilde{H}_{j}(|S_{<s}|;\Bbbk).

Therefore
\mu_{j}(s,\mathfrak{F}_{\min})=\dim\tilde{H}_{j}(|S_{<s}|;\Bbbk)
which is the predicted lower bound for the multiplicity. Theorem is finally proved.
∎

Remark C.78
.

Notice, that the problem of computing chains representing a basis of homology is outsourced to an external solver HomologyBasis. If the solver can deal with integral matrices, and if it is known that homology of
|S_{<s}|
are torsion-free, then it is natural to require that the solver outputs not just any basis of homology, but an integral basis, i.e. the set of integral generators. However, things become more tricky if
|S_{<s}|
has torsion in homology: in this case more generators are required to kill the homology. It is still possible to construct an integral analogue of the functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
algorithmically, however, we postpone such description for the future research.

Remark C.79
.

In the case of coefficients in a field, it is natural to define some optimization priors on the work of the homology solver. Notice that the solver should output the generators of a homology group
H_{j}(C|_{<s})
which are classes of chains of the corresponding complex. There is a freedom to choose the representatives. A natural prior would be to require the sparsity of the resulting representative chains. The more sparse is the incidence matrix, the easier is the subsequent (co)homology computation. This leads to an interesting practical problem in applied linear algebra.

Problem 8
.

Consider a couple of vector subspaces
B\subset Z\subset C=\Bbbk^{n}
. Find vectors
c^{(1)},\ldots,c^{(k)}\in C
such that

(1)

the set
\{[c^{(1)}],\ldots,[c^{(k)}]\}
is a basis of
Z/B
,

(2)

the total number
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\#\{j\in[n]\mid c_{j}^{(i)}\}
of vanishing components is maximized.

An algorithm, which effectively solves Problem
8
should be preferred in a system which computes cohomology of sheaves.

Remark C.80
.

Recalling Example
C.55
, we see a freedom in the definition of the incidence numbers, namely, we can set
\partial(b)=k_{1}\mathbf{1}+k_{2}\mathbf{2}-\mathbf{3}
with any choice of parameters
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
k_{1}+k_{2}=1
, and it leads to the honest cohomology computations. According to Remark
C.79
, we should prefer sparse solutions, which are either
(k_{1},k_{2})=(1,0)
or
(k_{1},k_{2})=(0,1)
. Notice that these two ways of defining a differential neatly correspond to the two geometrically meaningful ways of turning a Morse poset
\mathcal{Y}
into a cell poset, see Fig.
8
. In certain sense, the requirement of sparsity of solutions at each step somehow resembles cellular approximation theorem — in a homological language.

Remark C.81
.

It follows from Algorithm
1
that, whenever
|S_{<s}|
is acyclic, there are no generators
g
related to the element
s
. This means that the stalk
D(s)
does not contribute to
C_{\min}^{*}(S;D)
at all. In particular, it follows that cohomology of any sheaf
D
do not depend on the stalk
D(s)
with acyclic downset
D_{<s}
. This is well-aligned with Remark
C.58
.

For convenience we formulate an algorithm to compute cohomology of arbitrary diagram
D
on a finite poset
S
.

Construction C.82
.

First of all, we encode a diagram
D
by specifying the following data structures

(1)

for each
s\in S
, a finite set
N_{s}
is specified. This corresponds to a basis of the stalk
D(s)
;

(2)

for each pair
s,t\in S
such that
s<t
in
S
, a function
D_{st}\colon N_{s}\times N_{t}\to\Bbbk
is specified. This function encodes the matrix of the operator
D(s<t)
written in the chosen bases.

Notice that there are many redundant matrices in this structure: since the compositionality holds for a sheaf, it is sufficient to define the matrices
D_{st}
only for the edges
(s,t)
of the Hasse diagram of
S
(see Construction
2.9
), while all other matrices can be recovered as their products. However, it will be more convenient to work with this redundant structure: the several-hop matrices

D(s_{0}<\cdots<s_{k})=D(s_{k-1}<s_{k})\cdots D(s_{0}<s_{1})

may appear explicitly in the calculation.

It will be assumed that the set
G=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}G_{s}
of generating elements, and the incidence matrix
I\colon G\times G\to\Bbbk
are already precomputed by Algorithm
1
. The following algorithm is mathematically straightforward: we form the structure
C^{\prime}
of type
\CochCpx
representing the cochain complex
(C^{*}_{\min}(S;D),d_{\min})
, send send it to HomologyBasis which outputs the set of cochains representing cohomology generators together with their degrees. Recall from Construction
C.77
that
C^{\prime}
is a triple
(N,\deg^{\prime},\Dif)
, where
N
is a finite set of basic vectors,
\deg\colon N\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is the degree function, and
\Dif\colon N\times N\to\Bbbk
is the matrix of the differential, assumed to shift degrees by
+1
.

As follows from the definition of the cochain complex, we have
N=\bigsqcup_{g\in G}N_{\pr_{S}(g)}
. Again, given
n\in N
, the notation
\pr_{G}(n)\in G
stands for the corresponding index, and
\pr_{N}(n)\in N_{\pr_{S}(g)}
the element of the corresponding component.

Algorithm 2

Sheaf cohomology computation

1:

set

N
equal to
\bigsqcup_{g\in G}N_{\pr_{S}(g)}

2:

for
each
n
in
N

do

3:

set

\deg(n)
equal to
\deg(\pr_{G}(n))

4:

end

for

5:

for
each
(n_{1},n_{2})
in
N\times N

do

6:

set

\Dif(n_{1},n_{2})
equal to
I(\pr_{G}(n_{2}),\pr_{G}(n_{1}))\cdot D_{\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{1}))\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{2}))}(\pr_{N}(n_{1}),\pr_{N}(n_{2}))

7:

end

for

8:

define

C^{\prime}=(N,\deg,\Dif)
of type
\CochCpx

9:

procedure

(H^{\prime},\deg,RC^{\prime})
=HomologyBasis(
C^{\prime}
)

10:

output

(H,\deg,RC)

Notice that the matrix
\Dif
is well defined. The factor

D_{\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{1}))\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{2}))}(\pr_{N}(n_{1}),\pr_{N}(n_{2}))

is undefined when
\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{1}))\nless\pr_{S}(\pr_{G}(n_{2}))
. However, in this case the factor

I(\pr_{G}(n_{2}),\pr_{G}(n_{1}))

vanishes, as guaranteed by Algorithm
1
, so their product is set equal to
0
.

Remark C.83
.

Notice that Theorems
C.74
and
C.76
do not guarantee that, given a poset
S
and a sheaf
D
, the complex
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}(D)
is the most optimal way to compute cohomology of
D
. As stupid example: if
D
identically vanishes, the cohomology vanish as well, so there is no need to compute matrices
I
and
\Dif
. Instead, the theorems assert that, given a poset
S
, the complex
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}(D)
is the most optimal for all possible diagrams
D
on
S
. So the application of Algorithms
1
and
2
makes sense in the context when
S
is given, and there is a need to compute cohomology of several possible sheaves on
S
; the matrix
I
depends only on
S
hence can be reused in the calculations.

Certainly, in specific situation, the matrix
I
can be found without using Algorithm
1
. For example, if
S
is known to be a simplicial complex, the incidence numbers are defined in the alternating fashion, as in Construction
C.23
, which doesn’t require the calculation of homology of the down-sets. Algorithm
1
is applicable to any finite poset, hence we call it structure agnostic.

The development of algorithms which efficiently compute cohomology for a single diagram
D
on
S
is a challenging problem, which is a subject of the future work.

Appendix D
Laplacians, diffusion, and beyond

In this section we restrict to the category
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect}
of real vector spaces. The field
\mathbb{R}
has two important features, distinguishing it from general fields
\Bbbk
and making it suitable for practical applications. (1) It is ordered. (2) It is a complete metric space. By utilizing the first property one can define the general combinatorial Hodge theory. The second property leads to heat diffusion on sheaves which underlies the usage of sheaves in the design of neural networks. Most mathematical claims about Laplacians follow from the basic linear algebra reviewed in the next subsection.

D.1.
Linear algebra preliminaries

A euclidean space is a real vector space
V\in\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect}
with the chosen inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Each (finite-dimensional) euclidean space
V
is naturally identified with its dual
V^{*}
via the isomorphism
v\mapsto\langle v,\cdot\rangle
. Any map
f\colon V\to W
between euclidean spaces induces the conjugate map
f^{*}\colon W=W^{*}\to V=V^{*}
. If a map
f
is written as a matrix
F
in some orthogonal basis, then its conjugate
F^{*}
is written with the transpose matrix
F^{t}
. If
V=\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the arithmetical space, then we assume the scalar product is chosen in a standard way
\langle v,u\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}u_{i}
.

The next lemma is the classical statement, to be found in any elementary book on linear algebra.

Lemma D.1
.

For a linear map
f\colon V\to W
, we have
\Ker f=\Ker f^{*}f
.

Proof.

If
fv=0
, then
f^{*}fv=0
, so the inclusion
\Ker f\subseteq\Ker f^{*}f
is obvious. On the converse, if
f^{*}fv=0
, then

0=\langle f^{*}fv,v\rangle=\langle fv,fv\rangle
, which implies
fv=0
since the inner product is chosen non-degenerate.
∎

Construction D.2
.

Recall that an operator
A\colon V\to V
on euclidean space is called self-adjoint (symmetric matrix) if
A^{*}=A
. Every operator of the form
f^{*}f
or
gg^{*}
is obviously self-adjoint. The sum of self-adjoint operators is self-adjoint. The spectral theorem asserts that any self-adjoint operator can be diagonalized, i.e. there exists an orthogonal basis of
V
in which
A
is written as a diagonal matrix
\Lambda=\diag(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
. In other words, for any real symmetric matrix
A
there exists a spectral decomposition
A=Q^{t}\Lambda Q
where
Q
is orthogonal, and
\Lambda
is diagonal. The diagonal entries of
\Lambda
are eigenvalues of
A
, and the columns of
Q
are the eigenvectors of
A
. The problem of finding
Q
and
\Lambda
for a given symmetric matrix
A
is called the symmetric diagonalization problem. It can be solved by approximate algorithms of numerical linear algebra with floating point, see e.g.
[
190
]
.

Construction D.3
.

An operator
A\colon V\to V
is called nonnegative
29
29

29

We use this term as a shorter version of a more standard “positive semidefinite”.
, if
\langle Av,v\rangle\geq 0
. The proof of Lemma
D.1
shows that any operator of the form
f^{*}f
or
gg^{*}
is nonnegative. The sum of nonnegative operators is easily seen to be nonnegative. If
A
is self-adjoint, then
A
is nonnegative if and only if all its eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
are nonnegative real numbers. It can be seen from the spectral theorem, that if
A
is nonnegative self-adjoint, then

(D.1)

\Ker A=\{v\in V\mid\langle Av,v\rangle=0\}.

An important observation follows from formula (
D.1
). With any nonnegative symmetric matrix
A
, we can associate a function
Q_{A}\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
,
Q_{A}(x)=\langle Ax,x\rangle
, which is (1) quadratic, (2) nonnegative, (3) convex, (4) most importantly,

(D.2)

Q_{A}(x)=0\Leftrightarrow x\in\Ker A.

Hence the problem of finding a solution to the equation
Ax=0
can be faithfully reformulated as a problem of minimization of
Q_{A}
.

Notice that, whenever
A=f^{*}f
, we get

(D.3)

Q_{A}(x)=\langle x,f^{*}fx\rangle=\|fx\|^{2}.

Remark D.4
.

If
A_{1},A_{2}
are two nonnegative self-adjoint operators, then so is
A_{1}+A_{2}
. Since
Q_{A_{1}+A_{2}}=Q_{A_{1}}+Q_{A_{2}}
, it easily follows from the considerations of Construction
D.3
, that

\Ker(A_{1}+A_{2})=\Ker A_{1}\cap\Ker A_{2},

whenever
A_{1},A_{2}
are nonnegative self-adjoint.

With all this been written, the next important lemma is not much more complicated than Lemma
D.1
. It constitutes the basis of Hodge theory in the finite-dimensional case.

Lemma D.5
.

For a sequence of two linear maps
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}W
between euclidean spaces define an operator
L=f^{*}f+gg^{*}\colon V\to V
. Then we have an isomorphism

\Ker L\cong\Ker f/(\Ker f\cap\im g).

Proof.

Both
L_{1}=gg^{*}
and
L_{2}=f^{*}f
are nonnegative self-adjoint operators on
V
. We have

(D.4)

\Ker L=\Ker(L_{2}+L_{1})=\Ker L_{2}\cap\Ker L_{1}=\Ker f\cap\Ker g^{*}=\Ker f\cap(\im g)^{\bot}.

Whenever
A,B
are two subspaces of the euclidean space
V
, we have an isomorphism
A\cap B^{\bot}\cong A/(A\cap B)
, given by
a\mapsto[a]
. Therefore, the rightmost space in (
D.4
) is naturally isomorphic to
\Ker f/(\Ker f\cap\im g)
.
∎

D.2.
Laplacians and combinatorial Hodge theory

Recall the definition of cochain complex from subsection
C.1
.

Definition D.6
.

Consider a connective cochain complex
(C^{*},d)
in the category
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect}
:

0\to C^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{0}}}{{\to}}C^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\to}}C^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}\cdots

Assume an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is somehow chosen on every vector space
C_{i}
. Then we can define the sequence of nonnegative operators:

\Delta_{j}=d_{j}^{*}d_{j}+d_{j-1}d_{j-1}^{*}\colon C^{j}\to C^{j},

called
Laplace operators (or Laplacians)
. The vector subspace
\mathcal{H}_{j}=\Ker\Delta_{j}
is called
the harmonic subspace
of
C^{j}
, and its elements —
harmonic cochains
.

We have a natural corollary of Lemma
D.5

Corollary D.7
.

The
j
-th harmonic subspace of the euclidean cochain complex
(C^{*},d)
is (non-naturally) isomorphic to its
j
-th cohomology module of
(C^{*},d)
:

(D.5)

\mathcal{H}_{j}\cong H^{j}(C^{*},d).

Construction D.8
.

Let
D
be a cellular sheaf defined on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
, see Definitions
A.26
and
C.40
. Assume an inner product is chosen on each stalk
D(\sigma)
. This gives a canonical inner product on the cellular cochain spaces
C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)=\bigoplus_{\dim\sigma=j}D(\sigma)
, where direct summands are assumed orthogonal.

Definition D.9
.

The Laplacian
\Delta_{j}
of the cochain complex
(C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D),d_{CW})
is called the
j
-th cellular sheaf Laplacian
.

The assumptions needed to define euclidean structure on the cellular cochain complex can be generalized in a natural way.

Definition D.10
.

A
euclidean sheaf
on a poset
S
is a sheaf (diagram)
D
together with a choice of inner product on each stalk
D(s)
,
s\in S
.

Construction D.11
.

Let
D
be a euclidean sheaf on
S
. Let
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})
be a concrete functor in the sense of Definition
C.56
. Then the cochain complex
(C^{*},d)=\mathfrak{F}(D)
naturally attains the inner product. Indeed, each graded component
C^{j}
is a direct sum of stalks, taken with some multiplicities:

(D.6)

C^{j}=\mathfrak{F}(D)^{j}=\bigoplus_{s\in S}D(s)^{\oplus\mu_{j}(s,\mathfrak{F})}.

The inner product on
C^{j}
is defined by setting all direct summands pairwise orthogonal, while the inner product in each summand is summand
D(s)
is given by assumption.

Since we have a euclidean structure on
(C^{*},d)
, we can define
\mathfrak{F}
-based Laplacians
in the natural way:

(D.7)

\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}\colon C^{j}\to C^{j},\qquad\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}=d^{*}d+dd^{*}.

Recall that a concrete functor
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})
is said to honestly compute sheaf cohomology, if cohomology of
\mathfrak{F}(D)
is isomorphic to the sheaf cohomology, see Definition
C.50
. This definition and Corollary
D.7
easily imply the next statement.

Proposition D.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(S;\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})\to\mathbf{Cochain}(\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})
be a concrete functor that honestly computes sheaf cohomology, and let
D
be a euclidean sheaf on a poset
S
. Then

\Ker\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}\cong H^{j}(S;D).

Remark D.13
.

It follows from Construction
D.3
, that the linear subspace
\Ker\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}
coincides with the zero-set of the nonnegative quadratic function
Q_{\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}}
defined on the space
\mathfrak{F}(D)^{j}
, described in (
D.6
). Harmonic
j
-cochains can be found by minimizing this function. In particular, the space of global sections
\Gamma(S;D)\cong H^{0}(\mathfrak{F}(D))
can be found by minimizing the function

Q_{\Delta_{0}^{\mathfrak{F}}}(x)=\|d_{0}x\|^{2},

according to (
D.3
). Here
d_{0}\colon C^{0}(S;D)\to C^{1}(S;D)
is the leftmost differential in the cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}(D)
.

D.3.
Normalization

Computing cohomology reducing to finding
\Ker L_{j}
which can be done by diagonalizing the matrix
L
. However, when it comes to the study of Laplacians, the nonnegative symmetric matrices
L_{j}
are valuable and informative in their own right. Sometimes, the matrix
L_{j}
is replaced with another matrix
L_{j}^{\prime}
, which is better behaved from computational perspective, but preserves the main properties of
L_{j}
, namely, (1)
\Ker L_{j}^{\prime}=\Ker L_{j}
, (2)
L_{j}^{\prime}
is nonnegative symmetric. Such matrix
L_{j}^{\prime}
are usually called
normalizations
of
L_{j}
. This notion originated in spectral graph theory
[
51
]
, where many properties of general graphs are more naturally formulated in terms of eigenvalues of
L_{j}^{\prime}
rather than eigenvalues of
L_{j}
. It should be noticed however, that in the case of general sheaves, a normalization can be performed in a variety of ways, which we review below in the case of cellular sheaves.

Construction D.14
.

Consider a cellular sheaf
D
on a cell poset
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\sigma\in\mathcal{X}
be a cell. Let
k_{\sigma}
denote the dimension of a stalk
D(\sigma)
. Assume that an orthogonal basis
v_{\sigma,1},\ldots,v_{\sigma,k_{\sigma}}
is chosen in each stalk
D(\sigma)
. Then the collection of vectors
\bigsqcup_{\sigma\colon\dim\sigma=j}\{v_{\sigma,1},\ldots,v_{\sigma,k_{\sigma}}\}
becomes an orthogonal basis of
C^{j}_{CW}(\mathcal{X};D)
, for each
j\geq 0
. In this basis, the Laplacian
\Delta_{j}
is written as a symmetric nonnegatively defined matrix, which we denote by
L_{j}
in order to distinguish from the corresponding operator. Notice, that
L_{j}
has a natural block structure; given two cells
\sigma,\tau
of dimension
j
, we denote by
(L_{j})_{\sigma,\tau}
the block of
L_{j}
formed by the intersection of columns indexed by
(\sigma,1),\ldots,(\sigma,k_{\sigma})
and rows indexed by
(\tau,1),\ldots,(\tau,k_{\tau})
(i.e. the matrix corresponding to
\Delta_{j}
restricted to
D(\sigma)
in the domain, and
D(\tau)
in the target space).

(1)

Naive coordinate-wise normalization. Consider the diagonal matrix
K
filled with the values

K_{(\sigma,i),(\sigma,i)}=\begin{cases}(L_{j})_{(\sigma,i),(\sigma,i)}^{-1},&\mbox{if }(L_{j})_{(\sigma,i),(\sigma,i)}\neq 0\\
1,&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}

— i.e. the pseudoinverse of the diagonal part of
L_{j}
. Then the matrix

(D.8)

L_{j}^{\prime}=K^{1/2}L_{j}K^{1/2}

is symmetric nonnegative, has the same kernel as
L_{j}
, but the diagonal of
L_{j}^{\prime}
is only filled with
0
’s and
1
’s. We call
L_{j}^{\prime}
the weakly normalized sheaf Laplacian.

(2)

Stalk-wise normalization. Let
\sigma
be a cell of
\mathcal{X}
, and
A_{\sigma}=(L_{j})_{\sigma,\sigma}
be the corresponding diagonal block of
L_{j}
. Since
\Delta_{j}
is a nonnegative operator, so is its restriction to the subspace
D(\sigma)
, therefore the symmetric matrix
A_{\sigma}
is a matrix of a nonnegative operator. By the orthogonal diagonalization, there exists an orthogonal matrix
Q_{\sigma}
such that
Q_{\sigma}^{t}A_{\sigma}Q_{\sigma}
is a diagonal matrix
\diag(\lambda_{(\sigma,1)},\ldots,\lambda_{(\sigma,k_{\sigma})})
with nonnegative entries. Again, consider the pseudoinverse
N_{\sigma}=\diag(n_{(\sigma,1)},\ldots,n_{(\sigma,k_{\sigma})})
, where
n_{(\sigma,i)}=1/\lambda_{(\sigma,i)}^{-1}
if the denominator is nonzero, and
1
otherwise. Let us form a block diagonal orthogonal matrix
Q
with blocks
Q_{\sigma}
and diagonal matrix
N
with blocks
N_{\sigma}
. Then the matrix

(D.9)

L^{\prime\prime}_{j}=N^{1/2}Q^{t}LQ

is symmetric nonnegative, has the same kernel as
L_{j}
, but all the diagonal blocks
(L^{\prime\prime}_{j})_{\sigma,\sigma}
are diagonal matrices of the form
\diag(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
. We call
L^{\prime\prime}_{j}
the strongly normalized Laplacian.

(3)

We may take a matrix
M
which is pseudoinverse of
L_{j}
itself. Then
L_{j}^{\prime\prime\prime}=K^{1/2}L_{j}K^{1/2}
is a matrix of the form
\diag(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
. The computation of
M
is equivalent to diagonalization of
L_{j}
, so this case is usually not considered a normalization, despite the fact it is completely analogous to the previous two examples. However, we find it amusing that the study of sheaf Laplacians has explicit hierarchical structure.

D.4.
Heat diffusion

Assume a nonnegative self-adjoint operator
A\colon V\to V
is defined on a euclidean vector space
V
. According to Construction
D.3
the question of finding
\Ker A
is equivalent to minimizing the corresponding quadratic function
Q_{A}\colon V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
. The latter can be done e.g. by means of gradient descent.

Construction D.15
.

Consider a function
Q_{A}(x)=\langle Ax,x\rangle
defined on a finite-dimensional euclidean vector space. Its gradient equals

(D.10)

\nabla Q_{A}(x_{0})=(A+A^{*})x_{0}.

In the following, it will be assumed that
A
is self-adjoint, so that
\nabla Q_{A}=2A
. Henceforth, the continuous gradient descent flow of
Q_{A}
with a parameter
\eta>0
takes the form

(D.11)

\dot{x}=-2\eta Ax,\qquad x(0)=x_{0},

this is a linear system of differential equations. Its solution is
x(t)=\exp(-2\eta At)x_{0}
. If
A
is nonnegative, then there exists
x_{+\infty}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}x(t)
and it lies in
\Ker A
.

Similarly, the discrete gradient descent for
Q_{A}(x)
has the form

(D.12)

x_{k+1}=x_{k}-2\eta Ax_{k}\qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots.

The solution of this cascade is
x_{k}=(1-2\eta A)^{k}x_{0}
. Again, for nonnegative
A
, there exists
x_{+\infty}=\lim_{k\to+\infty}x_{k}
and this vector lies in
\Ker A
.

Estimation of the convergence rate of either continuous or discrete gradient flow is a straightforward exercise, but it emphasizes the role of eigenvalues of
A
.

Construction D.16
.

If
A=Q^{t}\Lambda Q
is a spectral decomposition with the diagonal matrix
\Lambda=\diag(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
, as in Construction
D.2
, then the solution to equation (
D.11
) has the form

x(t)=\exp(-2\eta At)x_{0}=Q^{t}\diag(e^{-2\eta\lambda_{1}t},\ldots,e^{-2\eta\lambda_{n}t})Qx_{0}.

Assume that
A
is nonnegative, i.e.
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
. As
t\to+\infty
, the exponent
e^{-2\eta\lambda_{i}t}
is constant whenever
\lambda_{i}=0
, and decreases to
0
when
\lambda_{i}>0
. The exponent with the least nonzero
\lambda_{i}
has the slowest convergence rate.

Similarly, the solution to a cascase (
D.12
) has the form

x_{k}=Q^{t}\diag((1-2\eta\lambda_{1})^{k},\ldots,(1-2\eta\lambda_{n})^{k})Qx_{0},

and the main term of the asymptotics is given by the one with the smallest positive
\lambda_{i}
. These considerations prove the following statement.

Proposition D.17
.

Let
A\neq 0
be nonnegative self-adjoint operator, and
\lambda_{\min}>0
be its least positive eigenvalue. Then the convergence rate of the equation (
D.11
) is asymptotically equivalent to
e^{-2\eta\lambda_{\min}t}
, for a generic initial value
x_{0}
. The rate of convergence of the cascade (
D.12
) is asymptotically equivalent to
(1-2\eta\lambda_{\min})^{k}
.

It remains to apply these standard arguments to sheaf Laplacians or their normalized versions. Let
\mathfrak{F}
be a concrete functor which honestly computes cohomology, let
\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}
be the
\mathfrak{F}
-based Laplacian of a euclidean sheaf
D
defined in Construction
D.11
, let
L_{j}
be a matrix of this Laplacian (in some orthogonal basis), and
L_{j}^{\prime}
— its normalized version.

Definition D.18
.

The function
Q_{j}(x)=\langle L_{j}^{\prime}x,x\rangle
is called
the (
\mathfrak{F}
-based,
j
-th order, normalized, Dirichlet) energy
of the euclidean sheaf
D
. Its gradient descent flow with parameter
\eta
, — either continuous

\dot{x}=-2\eta L_{j}^{\prime}(x),\qquad x(0)=x_{0},

or discrete

x_{k+1}=(1-2\eta L_{j}^{\prime})x_{k},\qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots

— is called a
heat diffusion
on a sheaf
D
.

By definition, the heat diffusion is the process that minimizes the sheaf’s energy, which is physically meaningful. Proposition
D.17
implies the following.

Corollary D.19
.

The convergence rate of the heat diffusion on a sheaf
D
is determined asymptotically by the least nonzero eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
of the corresponding (normalized) sheaf Laplacian
L_{j}^{\prime}
.

Remark D.20
.

The zero eigenspace of
L_{j}^{\prime}
coincides with
\Ker\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{F}}
(see Construction
D.14
) which is isomorphic to
H^{j}(S;D)
(see Proposition
D.12
). Therefore, the multiplicity of
0
-th eigenvalue of
L_{j}^{\prime}
equals the
j
-th Betti number of a sheaf
D
, i.e.
\dim H^{j}(S;D)
. The smallest positive eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}
measures “the worst” asymptotical term of the heat diffusion process. Other eigenvalues of
L_{j}^{\prime}
contain information on lower terms of the asymptotics for the heat diffusion process. It is believed that these eigenvalues reflect some geometrical properties of the underlying poset
S
, which is supported by a variety of results in spectral graph theory
[
51
]
. It should be noticed however, that there are no mathematical results relating eigenvalues of higher order Laplacians with higher dimensional discrete structures, such as simplicial complexes and cell posets.

D.5.
Examples of diffusion

We end up with two main examples which demonstrate how the terminology and results introduced in the previous subsections motivate particular constructions used in topological deep learning. The definitions of sheaf diffusion and message passing were given in the main part of the text, see subsection
2.7
. Here we use cellular cochain complex and Roos cochain complex described in subsections
C.5
and
C.3
respectively.

Diffusion in cellular sheaves.
Let
S
be a cell poset, e.g. a poset given by a simple graph, as in Example
2.2
. In this case, cellular cochain complex
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}(D)=(C^{*}_{CW}(S;D),d_{CW})
is defined and honestly computes cohomology.

Construction D.21
.

By Theorem
C.48
, we have

\Gamma(S;D)\cong H^{0}(S;D)\cong H^{0}(C^{*}_{CW}(S;D),d_{CW})=\Ker d_{CW}\colon C^{0}_{CW}(S;D)\to C^{1}_{CW}(S;D).

This formula shows that global sections of
D
can be defined as a subspace of
C^{0}_{CW}(S;D)=\bigoplus_{\rk s=0}D(s)
. For any element
s\in S
of rank 1 (that is an edge), the poset
S_{<s}
is by definition homeomorphic to a
0
-dimensional sphere, i.e. a pair of points. In other words, in a cell poset, each edge
s
has two endpoints
30
30

30

This should not surprise anyone who ever seen an interval.
, vertices
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
. Coherence relations reduce to the linear system
d_{CW}x=0
given by

f_{s^{\prime}s}(x_{s^{\prime}})-f_{s^{\prime\prime}s}(x_{s^{\prime\prime}})=0\mbox{ for each edge }s\mbox{ with endpoints }s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime},

where
f_{ts}=D(t<s)
denote the structure maps of the diagram
D
. The two signs in the above formula alternate, because only this choice of signs (incidence numbers) guarantees that
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}
honestly computes cohomology, in particular in degree 0. A global section can be found by minimizing the sheaf energy function
Q_{\Delta_{0}^{\mathfrak{F}_{CW}}}
corresponding to the functor
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}
; we denote it by
Q
for simplicity:

Q\colon C^{0}_{CW}(S;D)\to\mathbb{R},\qquad Q(x)=\sum_{s\colon\rk s=1}\|f_{s^{\prime}s}(x_{s^{\prime}})-f_{s^{\prime\prime}s}(x_{s^{\prime\prime}})\|^{2}.

See Remark
D.13
for the derivation of this expression.

Notice that, even in the case
\dim|S|>1
, i.e. if a cell poset
S
has elements other than vertices and edges, the global sections of a sheaf are defined through vertices and edges alone, see Remark
C.49
. The variables used in the system live over the vertices of
S
, while the edges are needed to define relations, and to perform linear message passing.

Remark D.22
.

The situation with Morse cell posets described in subsection
C.7
is completely similar, but the functor
\mathfrak{F}_{M}
is used instead of
\mathfrak{F}_{CW}
. This gives a definition of sheaf diffusion on non-graphical structures similar to the one shown on Fig.
8
.

Diffusion in binary relations.
Let
R\subseteq A\times B
be a binary relation (or a bipartite graph), which can be considered as a poset
S_{R}
on
A\sqcup B
, see Example
2.4
. In particular,
R
may encode a hypergraph, where
A
is the set of vertices,
B
is the set of hyperedges, and
R
is the relation of inclusion.

Construction D.23
.

A diagram
D
on
S_{R}
is, by the general definition, an assignment, to each vertex
a\in A
, a real vector space
D(a)
, to each vertex
b
, a space
D(b)
, and, whenever
aRb
, a linear map
f_{ab}\colon D(a)\to D(b)
. No compositionality restrictions are posed on these maps since
\dim|S_{R}|=1
.

The graded components of the Roos complex
C^{*}_{Roos}(S;D)
have the form:

C^{0}_{Roos}(S_{R};D)=\bigoplus_{a\in A}D(a)\oplus\bigoplus_{b\in B}D(b),\quad C^{1}_{Roos}(S;D)=\bigoplus_{ab\in R}D(b),

while the differential
d_{Roos}\colon C^{0}_{Roos}(S_{R};D)\to C^{1}_{Roos}(S_{R};D)
is defined by

d_{Roos}(x_{a})=-f_{ab}(x_{a})\mbox{ for }x_{a}\in D(a);\quad d_{Roos}(x_{b})=x_{b}\mbox{ for }x_{b}\in D(b).

All components
C^{j}_{Roos}(S;D)
with
j>1
vanish. The global sections
\Gamma(S_{R};D)\cong\Ker d_{Roos}
are the vectors
\bigoplus_{a\in A}x_{a}\oplus\bigoplus_{b\in B}x_{b}\in C^{0}_{Roos}(S_{R};D)
such that
f_{ab}(x_{a})=x_{b}
for any
aRb
. These are simply the coherence relations for the diagram
D
: this is not surprising due to Remark
C.30
.

The theory developed in subsection
D.2
claims that
\Ker d_{Roos}=\Ker\Delta
where
\Delta=d_{Roos}^{*}d_{Roos}
. The heat diffusion is given by minimization of Dirichlet’s energy
Q(x)=\langle x,\Delta x\rangle
given by

(D.13)

Q(x)=\sum_{ab\in R}\|x_{b}-f_{ab}(x_{a})\|^{2},

according to Remark
D.13
.

Example D.24
.

Assume that
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
,
B=\{b\}
is a singleton in Construction
D.23
, and the values
x_{a}\in D(a)
are forced to stay constant during the heat diffusion. Then minimization of the energy
Q
given by (
D.13
) makes
x_{b}
tend to the barycenter of
f(x_{a_{1}}),\ldots,f(x_{a_{m}})
, since the barycenter minimizes the sum of squared distances to a fixed finite set.

Remark D.25
.

In general, when
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\}
, each letter
b_{j}
can be treated as a hyperedge
A_{j}=\{a\mid aRb_{j}\}\subset A
. The minimization of
Q
makes the vectors
\{f_{ab_{j}}(x_{a})\mid a\in A_{j}\}
group around their barycenter
x_{b_{j}}
, for any hyperedge
b_{j}\in B
. The barycenters
x_{b}
can be removed from consideration: instead of minimizing
Q
given by (
D.13
), one can instead minimize the function

(D.14)

Q(x)=\sum_{b_{j}\in B}\frac{1}{|A_{j}|}\sum_{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in A_{j}}\|f_{a^{\prime}b_{j}}(x_{a^{\prime}})-f_{a^{\prime\prime}b_{j}}(x_{a^{\prime\prime}})\|^{2}.

The latter process makes the vectors
f_{ab_{j}}(x_{a})
group together for any hyperedge
A_{j}
. The above formula appears in the paper
[
70
]
introducing hypergraph sheaf neural networks. Essentially, this is the same function as (
D.13
) (at least if
x_{b}
is set equal to the barycenter of the corresponding vectors). Indeed, given
l
vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the sum of their pairwise squared distances equals
l
times the sum of squared distances to their barycenter. However, the formula (
D.13
) is a bit more optimal since it has fewer summands.

Remark D.26
.

The above construction utilizes Roos complex: it is permutation invariant in the sense that the formulas for the energy function
Q(x)
(either (
D.13
) or (
D.14
)) do not depend on the order of variables
a\in A_{j}
in each hyperedge
A_{j}
. It is possible to model the heat diffusion by using smaller number of summands. E.g. in Example
D.24
we could minimize the function
Q(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\|f_{a_{i+1}b}(x_{a_{i+1}})-f_{a_{i}b}(x_{a_{i}})\|^{2}
, the energy defined over a minimal functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
. However, this expression depends on the linear order of
a_{i}
’s, hence not permutation invariant anymore. Application of such formulas in constructions of neural architectures may be reasonable if the problem’s formulation subsumes some internal structure on hyperedges of a hypergraph.

Derived perspective on binary relations.
Treating hypergraphs as binary relations (or bipartite graphs) has a conceptual advantage: the symmetric role of vertices
A
and hyperedges
B
becomes more transparent. Given a relation
R\subset A\times B
, we can swap
A
and
B
and get a relation
R^{\op}\subset B\times A
, such that
bR^{\op}a\Leftrightarrow aRb
. The corresponding poset
S_{R^{\op}}
coincides with
S_{R}^{\op}
obtained from
S_{R}
by reversing the order. As obvious from the definition of geometrical realization, we have
|S_{R}^{\op}|=|S_{R}|
. A sheaf theoretical counterpart of this claim should be mentioned.

Remark D.27
.

It was proved in
[
128
, Cor.4.18]
that the derived categories
\mathscr{D}^{b}(S_{R})
and
\mathscr{D}^{b}(S_{R^{\op}})
are equivalent, see Remark
F.10
. This can be seen as an instance of a more general fact: Coxeter functors (see
[
25
]
) provide the derived equivalence of the categories of representations
[
139
, Thm.3.19]
. In the absence of compositionality restrictions, the category of quiver representations coincides with the category of sheaves on a poset which implies the claim.

The stated claim has a straightforward practical implication. Whenever a hypergraph is studied by homological methods, one can swap the roles of its elements: consider hyperedges as vertices, and vertices as hyperedges. The equivalence of derived categories guarantees that no essential information is lost. Such swap makes sense if calculations with
R^{\op}
are less costly than those with
R
.

Construction D.28
.

The construction of sheaves on a hypergraph is utilizing the poset
S_{R}
which is 1-dimensional; therefore all cohomology in degrees
>1
of all sheaves on
S_{R}
vanish, see Corollary
C.33
. However, in algebraic topology there is another way to transform a binary relation into a higher-order structure, the Dowker complex. A binary relation (a hypergraph) can be turned into a simplicial complex by treating hyperedges as simplices and adding their proper subsets. Dowker complex better resembles the intuitions behind applied papers, since hyperedges with
>2
vertices actually become higher-dimensional structures and there may be nontrivial higher-order cohomology.

Swapping the arguments of a binary relation leads to a Dowker duality. The simplicial complexes constructed from
R
and
R^{\op}
are homotopically equivalent; an elegant functorial proof of this claim can be found in
[
38
]
. Therefore, the homotopy type of a binary relation is well defined and symmetric in the arguments of the relation. It seems an interesting research problem: to define sheaf theory on binary relations in the way that remembers higher order structure of Dowker complex.

Diffusion in general.
For a general poset
S
, the somehow canonical way to define Laplacian and diffusion on sheaves on
S
is by using Roos complex
\mathfrak{F}_{Roos}
, since this construction is universal. This idea of combining Laplacians with general posets was proposed in
[
50
]
motivated by the particular case of binary relations and hypergraphs.

Another way to introduce Laplacians and diffusion over arbitrary posets is by using a minimal functor
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
. Let
S_{low}
denote the subset of minimal elements of
S
. It can be seen from the inductive construction of
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
presented in subsection
C.8
, that
C^{0}_{\min}(S;D)=\bigoplus_{s\in S_{low}}D(s)
, and the number of summands of
C^{1}_{\min}(S;D)
equals, by Definition
C.70
, to the sum of multiplicities
\sum_{s\in S}\mu_{1}(s,\mathfrak{F}_{\min})
, i.e. the number of generators of degree
1
output by Algorithm
1
. The formula for the energy function
Q_{\Delta_{0}^{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}}
is determined by this generating set.

We finish with the general remark addressed to the specialists in deep learning.

Remark D.29
.

In general, the mathematical pipeline described in Appendix sections
C
and
D
, consists of the following steps

(1)

choice of cochain complex which honestly computes cohomology (or just global sections),

(2)

choice of euclidean structure on a sheaf,

(3)

choice of normalization for a Laplacian.

Each of this steps subsumes picking real parameters
31
31

31

Sometimes some discrete optimization is involved, like choosing the optimal dimensions of stalks, but in all cases there is a bunch of real numbers to optimize.
hence can be learned end-to-end by gradient descent, or constructed by hand based on the specifics of the problem at hand.

Example D.30
.

The mechanism of sheaf attention networks mentioned in Section
4
is an example of learning the euclidean structure. The standard euclidean structure on stalks of edges is scaled by the learnable attention weights.

Example D.31
.

If hyperedges of a hypergraph have an unknown internal structure, then it can be captured by the learnable cochain complex as outlined in Remark
D.26
. The only mathematical restriction on the complex is that it should honestly compute cohomology, otherwise the whole theory doesn’t make much sense.

Appendix E
Connection sheaves

In this section we briefly recall the basic notions related to connection sheaves. Here, similar to Appendices
C
and
D
,
\mathbf{V}
is an abelian category, with
\mathbf{V}=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect}
being the main example. For a more detailed overview of the mathematical results related to posets we refer to
[
23
]
.

Definition E.1
.

A
\mathbf{V}
-valued diagram
D
on a poset
S
(a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
on an Alexandrov space
X_{S}
) is called a
connection sheaf
if all structure maps
D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})
are isomorphisms in
\mathbf{V}
. More generally, let
G
be a subgroup of the monoid
\Hom_{\mathbf{V}}(V,V)
for some
V\in\mathbf{V}
. Then a
G
-connection sheaf
on
S
is a diagram
D
in which all structure maps
D(s_{1}\leq s_{2})
belong to
G
.

In classical algebraic topology connection sheaves are known as local systems; they were introduced by Steenrod in
[
187
]
.

Remark E.2
.

Connection sheaf on a finite poset is a suitable discretization for the notion of a bundle over a manifold, such as tangent bundle of a smooth manifold. In a bundle, we have a real vector space of the same dimension attached to each point of a manifold. In order to take derivatives of tensor fields, the spaces attached to infinitely closed points are related by a certain isomorphism; this is formalized in the notion of connection from differential geometry. This basic and extremely powerful object of differential geometry leads to the notions of parallel transport and Riemann curvature tensor.

A straightforward way to discretize this setting is to take a poset
S
, assign, to each element
s\in S
, a real vector space
D(s)
of the same dimension, and, whenever
s_{1}<s_{2}
(which is treated as closeness of points) assign an isomorphism between
D(s_{1})
and
D(s_{2})
. This seemingly leads to Definition
E.1
of a sheaf. However, the analogy between connection sheaves on posets and differential connections on manifolds may be a bit misleading if a poset is more complicated than just a graph. In sheaf theory compositionality
D(s_{1}<s_{2});D(s_{2}<s_{3})=D(s_{1}<s_{3})
is required. In differential geometry, the discrepancy between “various ways to move around a point” measures the curvature and may be nontrivial. In this sense, connection sheaves may be seen as discrete models of flat manifolds. General quiver representations with isomorphic maps can model arbitrary manifolds.

Assume that
S
is connected (meaning that the geometrical realization
|S|
is a connected topological space). This means that for any two points
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\in S
there is a path

p=(s^{\prime}\lessgtr s_{1}\lessgtr s_{2}\lessgtr\cdots\lessgtr s_{n}\lessgtr s^{\prime\prime})

where
a\lessgtr b
denotes either
a\leq b
or
a\geq b
. The reversed path
p^{-1}
from
s^{\prime\prime}
to
s^{\prime}
is defined in a straightforward manner.

Construction E.3
.

Given a connection sheaf
D
on
S
and a path
p
between
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
as above, we can define
the parallel transport
operator

T_{p}\colon D(s^{\prime})\to D(s^{\prime\prime}),\qquad T_{p}=D(s^{\prime}\lessgtr s_{1});D(s^{\prime}\lessgtr s_{1});\cdots;D(s_{n}\lessgtr s^{\prime\prime}),

where we set by definition

D(a\lessgtr b)=\begin{cases}D(a\leq b),&\mbox{if }a\lessgtr b\mbox{ reads as }a\leq b\\
D(b\leq a)^{-1},&\mbox{if }a\lessgtr b\mbox{ reads as }a\geq b.\end{cases}

We obviously have
T_{p^{-1}}=T_{p}^{-1}
. The definition of a sheaf
2.13
directly implies
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{p^{\prime\prime}}
if a path
p^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from
p^{\prime}
by deletion of fragment
(s_{i}=s_{i+1})
or substituting a fragment
(s_{i-1}\leq s_{i}\leq s_{i+1})
with
(s_{i-1}\leq s_{i+1})
or similar operation for the reversed relation
\geq
, — we call such operations triangular reductions. Therefore, it can be assumed without loss of generality that a parallel transport is determined along paths composed of alternating strict inequalities. This argument proves the following.

Lemma E.4
.

If
p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}
are two homotopic paths from
s^{\prime}
to
s^{\prime\prime}
in
S
, then
T_{p^{\prime}}=T_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.

Proof.

Construction
E.3
shows that two paths differing by a triangle
(s_{0}<s_{1}<s_{2})
in
|\ord S|=|S|
give the same parallel transport operators. By the cellular approximation theorem, every homotopy between
s^{\prime}
to
s^{\prime\prime}
lies in a 2-skeleton of
|S|
. Since all 2-cells of
|S|
have the form
(s_{0}<s_{1}<s_{2})
a homotopy is a sequence of triangular reductions or their inverses, hence preserves the parallel transport operator.
∎

Remark E.5
.

If the initial and end-points coincide, a path from
s
to itself is called a loop, and its homotopy class is, by definition, the element of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(|S|,s)
. Lemma
E.4
implies that the parallel transport map
T_{p}
provides a well-defined homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(|S|,s)
to
\Iso(D(s))
, which is called a
monodromy
of a connection sheaf. In case
D
is
G
-connection sheaf, the monodromy is valued in the subgroup
G
. A connection sheaf is called
flat
if its monodromy is a trivial representation. This means that traversing any loop is identical on
D(s)
.

More generally, let
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
denote the full subcategory of connection sheaves in
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
. The next proposition and its proof are subsumed by the previous remark, and seem to be folklore.

Proposition E.6
.

For a connected poset
S
, the category
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
is equivalent to the category
\mathbf{Repr}(\pi_{1}(|S|),\mathbf{V})
of
\mathbf{V}
-valued representations of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(|S|)
.

Remark E.7
.

By McCord’s theorem we have
\pi_{1}(|S|)\cong\pi_{1}(X_{S})
, see Remark
A.17
. The claim of Proposition
E.6
can thus be formulated as an equivalence of the categories
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
and
\mathbf{Repr}(\pi_{1}(X_{S}),\mathbf{V})
.

In view of Remark
E.2
on relation between connection sheaves and connections in differential geometry, this proposition may be considered as a discrete version of Riemann–Hilbert correspondence (rather naive though). To prove it, we choose a base point
s_{0}\in S
and a spanning tree
G
of the 1-skeleton
|(\ord S)_{1}|
. For any point
s\in S
, there is a unique path
p_{s}
from
s_{0}
to
s
lying in
G
. These data will be assumed fixed. The following two constructions emulate the procedure of collapsing/decolapsing a spanning skeleton into one point, and prove Proposition
E.6
.

Construction E.8
.

Given a connection sheaf
D
on
S
, we construct a representation of
\pi_{1}(|S|,s_{0})
on the space
D(s_{0})
. For an element
g=[p]\in\pi_{1}(|S|,s_{0})
represented by a loop
p
from
s_{0}
to itself, set the action of
g
on
D(s_{0})
defined by
T_{p}
. This is well-defined on homotopy classes by Lemma
E.4
. This is a group homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(|S|,s_{0})
to
\End(D(s_{0}))
by the definition of the parallel transport.

Construction E.9
.

Given a fundamental group representation
R\colon\pi_{1}(|S|,s_{0})\to\End(V)
, we construct a connection sheaf
\bar{V}
on
S
. Set
\bar{V}(s)=V
for any
s
. Then for
s_{1}<s_{2}
set

\bar{V}(s_{1}<s_{2})=R([p_{s_{1}};(s_{1}<s_{2});p_{s_{2}}^{-1}])\colon V=\bar{V}(s_{1})\to V=\bar{V}(s_{2}),

where
[p_{s_{1}};(s_{1}<s_{2});p_{s_{2}}]\in\pi_{1}(|S|,s_{0})
is the homotopy class of the loop obtained by concatenation of the chosen path
p_{s_{1}}
from
s_{0}
to
s_{1}
, the shortcut from
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
, and the reversed chosen path
p_{s_{2}}^{-1}
from
s_{2}
to
s_{0}
. It is a simple exercise to prove that
\bar{V}
is a well-defined connection sheaf.

The proof that Constructions
E.8
and
E.9
are inverses to each other up to natural isomorphism (given the tree
G
) is rather straightforward and left as an exercise. Of course, a more conceptual and mathematically correct way to state Proposition
E.6
is via the fundamental groupoid of
|S|
.

Remark E.10
.

It should be noted that the category
\mathbf{Repr}(\pi_{1}(|S|),\mathbf{V})
(and hence
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
according to Proposition
E.6
) is an abelian category itself, and it has enough injectives if so does
\mathbf{V}
. However, the subcategory
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
does not inherit all its structure from
\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
. Injective sheaves on
S
may fail to be connection sheaves, and injective connection sheaves may fail to be injective sheaves of
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
. In particular, the notion of cohomology in
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbf{V})
and
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
vary. When speaking about cohomology of connection sheaves, one should be extremely careful in terminology.

Example E.11
.

As an example, consider the constant sheaf
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{1}}
on
S
. Its cohomology, as defined in the category
\mathbf{Diag}(S,\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect})
, coincides with the singular cohomology of the geometrical realization
H^{*}(|S|;\mathbb{R})
, as discussed in Example
C.32
. However,
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{1}}
is actually a connection sheaf,
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{1}}\in\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
. In terms of Proposition
E.6
it corresponds to the trivial
\pi_{1}(|S|)
-module
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, so its cohomology coincides with the group cohomology
H^{*}(\pi_{1}(|S|);\mathbb{R}^{1})
. The modules
H^{*}(|S|;\mathbb{R})
and
H^{*}(\pi_{1}(|S|);\mathbb{R}^{1})
may be different, e.g. if
|S|
is homeomorphic to 2-sphere.

Similarly, if
D
is an arbitrary connection sheaf, then
H^{*}(S;D)
computed in
\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
is the cohomology of
S
with local coefficients
D
, while
H^{*}(S;D)
computed in
\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
is the cohomology of the
\pi_{1}(S,s_{0})
-module
D(s_{0})
. In a sense, passage from the category
\mathbf{Diag}(S;\mathbf{V})
to the subcategory
