\mathbf{ConDiag}(S,\mathbf{V})
forgets higher homotopical information encoded in
S
.

We have the following simple corollary of Proposition
E.6
which may seem counterintuitive at first glance.

Corollary E.12
.

If
|S|
is simply connected, then every connection sheaf on
S
is isomorphic to the constant sheaf with the same stalks’ dimension.

Remark E.13
.

The natural question arises: if the connection sheaves are just the constant sheaf, then what’s the point in “learning” a connection sheaf as described in Subsection
2.7
and the Section
4
of the review? There are several reasonable answers.

(1)

Data structures, on which connection sheaf learning is performed are usually graphs, and graphs are rarely simply connected.

(2)

If data structures are highly dimensional, we actually learn connection quiver representations rather than ordinary connection sheaf and simply forget compositionality relations.

Anyhow, the question once again leads to Problem
2
from the list. There won’t be any actual higher-dimensional topology in deep learning, until the relations are taken into account.

Appendix F
Information capacity of sheaves

In this section we discuss to which extent the category of sheaves on a space remembers the space. All the claims made in this subsection are standard from mathematical perspective, but they rigorify the usage of sheaves in geometrical problems and provide a general fundament for sheaf learning. We couldn’t find the mentions of these claims in the literature, and add them in our review for completeness.

F.1.
Topos of sheaves

Given a topological space
X
and a chosen target category
\mathbf{V}
, we can form a category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{V})
of
\mathbf{V}
-valued sheaves on
X
. The space
X
is a bare geometrical structure, no computations are available on
X
itself. We need to borrow certain “types of computations” which exist in the target category
\mathbf{V}
, and put them into
\mathbf{Shvs}(S,\mathbf{V})
in order to be able to say something about
X
. The natural question arises: how much information do we loose, when passing from
X
to the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{V})
? In case
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{Sets}
this is a classical question and the answer is well-known.

Construction F.1
.

We refer to
[
141
]
for the definition of a topos. The category
\mathbf{Sets}
is a topos, and, for any topological space
X
, the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{Sets})
is also a topos. Given two topoi
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}
, a geometrical morphism
F
from
\mathbf{T}_{1}
to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
consists of a pair of adjoint functors
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{T}_{1}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{T}_{2}\colon f^{*}
such that
f^{*}
preserves finite limits. The class of all toposes with geometrical morphisms between them forms a category, which will be denoted
\mathbf{Topoi}
. A continuous map of topological spaces
f\colon X\to Y
, induces direct and inverse image functors
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{Sets})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Shvs}(Y;\mathbf{Sets})\colon f^{*}
as described in subsection
B.5
, they form a geometrical morphism in the category
\mathbf{Topoi}
. Hence, passing to a topos of
\mathbf{Sets}
-valued sheaves defines a functor

(F.1)

\mathbf{Shvs}(\cdot,\mathbf{Sets})\colon\mathbf{Top}\to\mathbf{Topoi},\quad X\mapsto\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{Sets}).

Topological space
X
is called
sober
if, roughly speaking, it can be reconstructed from its lattice, or locale,
\OpSets(X)
of open subsets. See formal definition e.g. in
[
141
, p.477]
.

Proposition F.2
.

The functor
\mathbf{Shvs}(\cdot,\mathbf{Sets})
is fully faithful on the full subcategory
\mathbf{Top}_{sober}
of sober topological spaces.

The proof follows from
[
141
, Cor.4,p.481]
and
[
141
, Prop.2, p.491]
. It is not surprising that the lattice
\OpSets(X)
is what actually matters: presheaves were defined as diagrams on
\OpSets(X)^{\op}
.

Corollary F.3
.

If
X,Y
are sober topological spaces, and
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{Sets})
is isomorphic as a topos to
\mathbf{Shvs}(Y,\mathbf{Sets})
, then
X\cong Y
.

Remark F.4
.

Unwinding the formal proof of Proposition
F.2
we can notice that the whole big category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\mathbf{Sets})
is not actually needed to recover a sober space
X
. Basically, the lattice
\OpSets(X)
coincides with the collection of subsheaves of the constant
\ast
-valued sheaf, up to isomorphism. Here
\ast
is the singleton, the final object of
\mathbf{Sets}
.

Remark F.5
.

Any finite
T_{0}
-space can be shown to be sober. Corollary
F.3
gives a strict mathematical ground for the belief that
\mathbf{Sets}
-valued diagrams over finite posets (resp., sheaves over corresponding
T_{0}
-spaces, according to Proposition
A.7
) store the complete information about the underlying topology. It should be noticed however, that not every Alexandrov
T_{0}
-space is sober: such space is sober if and only if the corresponding poset is Artinian
[
136
, Thm.C.6]
.

Remark F.6
.

If
S
is just a preordered set, and
\bar{S}
is its corresponding poset, as in Construction
A.4
, then we have
\OpSets(X_{S})\cong\OpSets(X_{\bar{S}})
. Therefore Alexandrov topology
X_{S}
and the corresponding
T_{0}
-topology
X_{\bar{S}}
are indistinguishable by means of topoi of sheaves. This is the main conceptual reason why we restrict consideration to posets, and not general preordered sets, see Remark
B.3
.

The natural question arises in abelian setting (see definition of abelian category in Appendix
C
). To which extent the abelian category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})
remembers a space
X
? This situation differs from the case of a topos: it appears that the abelian categories are not that “rigid” objects as topoi as shown in the next remark.

Remark F.7
.

The functor
\mathbf{Shvs}(\cdot,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})\colon\mathbf{Top}\to\mathbf{AbCat}
is not faithful (even on sober spaces). Indeed, there is exactly one continuous map from a singleton
\ast
to itself, but there are infinitely many additive endofunctors on
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
(which is identified with
\mathbf{Shvs}(\ast,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})
). E.g. we have an infinite series
V\mapsto V^{\oplus n}
.

It could be noticed that, for sheaves valued in an abelian category, both direct
f_{*}
and inverse image
f^{*}
maps are additive functors. We could wonder, if it is possible to make category
\mathbf{AbCat}
more rigid, by considering adjoint pairs of additive functors
(f_{*},f^{*})
as morphisms instead of a single additive morphism, i.e. by “simulating” a topos. However, this doesn’t resolve the previous counterexample: the functor
f_{*}\colon V\mapsto V^{\oplus n}
has an adjoint
f^{*}\colon V\mapsto V^{\oplus n}
.

Nevertheless, the category of
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
-diagrams on a poset still remembers a poset.

Proposition F.8
.

The locale
\OpSets(X)
can be recovered from
\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})
. The functor
\mathbf{Shvs}(\cdot,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})\colon\mathbf{Top}\to\mathbf{AbCat}
is full on sober spaces.

Proof.

We only need to prove the first claim, the second follows from the equivalence between the category of spatial locales and sober topological spaces, see
[
141
, Cor.4,p.481]
. There is a distinguished element
\Bbbk\in\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
, which is a unique up to isomorphism simple object of the abelian category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
. Hence we can construct a constant
\Bbbk
-valued sheaf
\bar{\Bbbk}\in\mathbf{Shvs}(X,\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect})
. Subsheaves of
\bar{\Bbbk}
, defined up to isomorphism, form a poset which is isomorphic to the locale
\OpSets(X)
.
∎

This proof is similar to Remark
F.4
, but now we use
\Bbbk
instead of
\ast
and
0
instead of
\varnothing
. This observation mimics the boolean logic (inherent to the topos
\mathbf{Sets}
) in terms of linear algebra (inherent to the abelian category
\Bbbk\mathbf{Vect}
).

Remark F.9
.

Proof of Proposition
F.8
shows that, from mathematical perspective, the complete information about finite
T_{0}
-topology can be extracted from sheaves, whose stalks are no more than 1-dimensional. This is somehow orthogonal to the deep learning practice, where it is assumed that a single sheaf with higher-dimensional stalks (or a few such sheaves used in the layers of a network) is already expressive enough to describe the properties of the space, see Section
4
. This discrepancy between theory and practice seems interesting and deserves further investigation.

Remark F.10
.

The topos
\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{Sets})
is interesting in its own right. However, when working in abelian setting, it is common in algebraic geometry to consider not the category
\mathbf{Shvs}(X;\mathbf{Abel})
of sheaves itself, but its derived versions which are better suited for homological applications. For a poset
S
, or the corresponding Alexandrov space
X_{S}
, the bounded derived category
\mathscr{D}^{b}(S)
of finite-dimensional sheaves is defined. A comprehensive study of derived categories of
\mathbf{Abel}
-valued sheaves on posets was done by Ladkani
[
128
]
. There exist examples of non-isomorphic finite posets which are derived-equivalent.

*
